# Jesus

**Jesus**[^cite_note-16] (c. 6 to 4 BC – AD 30 or 33), also known as **Jesus Christ**,[^cite_note-17] **Jesus of Nazareth**, and by various [other names and titles](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Names_and_titles_of_Jesus_in_the_New_Testament), was a 1st-century [Jewish](25955086.md) preacher and [religious leader](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lists_of_religious_leaders) in the [Roman province of Judaea](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Judaea_\(Roman_province\)).[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEVermes198120,_26,_27,_29-18] He is the [central figure](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jesus_in_Christianity) of [Christianity](5211.md), the [world's largest religion](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Major_religious_groups). Most [branches of Christianity](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Christian_denominations) consider Jesus the [incarnation](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Incarnation_\(Christianity\)) of [God the Son](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/God_the_Son) and the awaited [Messiah](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Messiah#Christianity) or [Christ](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Christ_\(title\)). Accounts of [Jesus's life](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Life_of_Jesus) are contained in the [Gospels](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gospel), especially the [four canonical Gospels](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Four_canonical_Gospels) of the [New Testament](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/New_Testament). Since [the Enlightenment](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Age_of_Enlightenment), [academic research](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Quest_for_the_historical_Jesus) has produced various views on the [historical reliability of the Gospels](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Historical_reliability_of_the_Gospels) and the extent to which they reflect the [historical Jesus](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Historical_Jesus),[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEPowell1998168–173-19][^cite_note-22][^cite_note-FOOTNOTEBowman_Jr.Komoszewski201922–23-23][^cite_note-24] but virtually all modern scholars of [antiquity](252905.md) agree that [Jesus existed historically](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Historicity_of_Jesus).[^cite_note-exist-32]

According to [Christian tradition](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Christian_tradition), as represented in the Gospels and the [Acts of the Apostles](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Acts_of_the_Apostles), Jesus [was circumcised](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Circumcision_of_Jesus) at eight days old, [presented](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Presentation_of_Jesus) at the [Temple in Jerusalem](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Temple_in_Jerusalem) at 40 days old, [baptized](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Baptism_of_Jesus) by [John the Baptist](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/John_the_Baptist) as a young adult, and, after [40 days and nights](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Temptation_of_Christ) of fasting in the wilderness, began [his public ministry](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ministry_of_Jesus). He was an [itinerant teacher](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Itinerant_teacher) whom his followers believed to possess divine authority in interpreting [Jewish law](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Halakha). Jesus often debated with other Jews, most commonly the [Pharisees](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pharisees) and [Sadducees](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sadducees), about how best to [follow God](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/God_in_Judaism), engaged in healings, taught in [parables](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Parables_of_Jesus), and gathered followers, 12 of whom he appointed as his [apostles](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Apostles_in_the_New_Testament). According to the New Testament accounts, he was arrested in [Jerusalem](16043.md) and [tried](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sanhedrin_trial_of_Jesus) by the [Sanhedrin](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sanhedrin),[^cite_note-FOOTNOTESanders199311-33] handed over to the Roman authorities, and [crucified](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Crucifixion_of_Jesus) on the order of [Pontius Pilate](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pontius_Pilate), the Roman prefect of [Judaea](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Judaea_\(Roman_province\)). In [Christian theology](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Christian_theology), the [Resurrection](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Resurrection_of_Jesus) is the event in which Jesus rose from the dead, on the third day after his death, and this belief became the foundation of the [early Christian Church](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Early_Christianity), which expanded into a [worldwide movement](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Spread_of_Christianity).[^cite_note-FOOTNOTESanders199311,_14-34]

Christian theology includes the beliefs that Jesus was conceived by the [Holy Spirit](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Holy_Spirit_in_Christianity), was [born of a virgin](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Virgin_birth_of_Jesus) named [Mary](73513.md), performed [miracles](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Miracles_of_Jesus), founded the [Christian Church](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Christian_Church), died by crucifixion as a sacrifice for [atonement for sin](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Atonement_in_Christianity), was resurrected by God the Father, and [ascended](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ascension_of_Jesus) into [Heaven](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Heaven_in_Christianity), from where he will return. Christians commonly believe that Jesus enables people to be [reconciled to God](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/God_in_Christianity). The [Nicene Creed](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nicene_Creed) asserts that Jesus will [judge the living and the dead](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Last_Judgment), either [before](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Intermediate_state_\(Christianity\)) or [after](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Christian_mortalism) their [bodily resurrection](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Resurrection_of_the_dead#Christianity), an event associated with the [Second Coming of Jesus](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Second_Coming) in [Christian eschatology](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Christian_eschatology). Many branches of Christianity consider Jesus as the incarnation of God the Son, the second of the three [persons](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Prosopon) of the [Trinity](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Trinity).[^cite_note-35] The [birth of Jesus](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Birth_of_Jesus) in [Bethlehem](4312.md) is celebrated annually, generally on 25 December,[^cite_note-36] as [Christmas](6237.md). His [crucifixion](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Crucifixion) is commemorated on [Good Friday](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Good_Friday) and his resurrection on [Easter Sunday](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Easter_Sunday). The world's most widely used [calendar era](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Calendar_era)—in which the current year is [AD](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Anno_Domini) 2026 (or 2026 [CE](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Common_Era))—is traditionally based on the approximate [date of the birth of Jesus](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Date_of_the_birth_of_Jesus).[^cite_note-37]

Mainstream Judaism [rejects the belief](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jewish_views_on_Jesus) that Jesus was the awaited messiah, holding that he did not fulfil [messianic prophecies](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Messiah_in_Judaism), was not lawfully [anointed](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Anointed), and was neither divine nor resurrected. In contrast, [Jesus in Islam](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jesus_in_Islam)[^cite_note-38] is considered the [messiah](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Messiah#Islam) and a [prophet](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Prophets_and_messengers_in_Islam) of [God](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/God_in_Islam), who was sent to the [Israelites](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Israelites) and [will return to Earth](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Second_Coming#Islam) before the [Day of Judgement](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Judgement_Day_in_Islam). [Muslims](19541.md) believe that Jesus was born of the virgin [Mary](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mary_in_Islam) but was neither God nor the son of God. Most Muslims [do not believe that he was killed or crucified](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Islamic_views_on_Jesus's_death), but that God [raised him into Heaven while he was still alive](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Entering_heaven_alive).[^cite_note-39] Jesus is also revered in the [Baháʼí](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bah%C3%A1%CA%BC%C3%AD_Faith) and [Druze faiths](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Druze_faith), as well as in [Rastafari](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rastafari).

## Name

Further information: [Jesus (name)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jesus_\(name\)), [Names and titles of Jesus in the New Testament](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Names_and_titles_of_Jesus_in_the_New_Testament), [Holy Name of Jesus](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Holy_Name_of_Jesus), and [Names of God in Christianity](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Names_of_God_in_Christianity)



From top-left: [Aramaic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Aramaic), [Hebrew](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hebrew), [Greek](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Greek_language), [Latin](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Latin), and English transcriptions of the name *Jesus*

A Jewish person in Jesus's time usually [had only one name](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jewish_name), sometimes followed by a [patronymic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Patronymic) phrase of the form "son of \[father's name]", or by the person's home town.[^cite_note-Britannica-40] Thus, in the New Testament, Jesus is commonly referred to as "Jesus of [Nazareth](21702.md)".[^cite_note-41] Jesus's neighbours in Nazareth referred to him as "the carpenter, the son of [Mary](73513.md) and brother of [James](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/James,_brother_of_Jesus) and [Joses](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Joses,_brother_of_James_the_younger) and [Judas](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jude,_brother_of_Jesus) and [Simon](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Simon,_brother_of_Jesus)", "the carpenter's son", or "[Joseph](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Saint_Joseph)'s son"; in the [Gospel of John](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gospel_of_John), the disciple [Philip](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Philip_the_Apostle) refers to him as "Jesus son of Joseph from Nazareth".[^cite_note-42]

The name *Jesus* is the English transliteration, through Latin *Iesus*, of [Ancient Greek](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ancient_Greek_language): Ἰησοῦς, which is the Greek rendering of the [Hebrew](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hebrew) name Joshua (יְהוֹשֻׁעַ‎ *Yehoshua*).[^cite_note-43][^cite_note-BensonMatthew1-44] The Hebrew/[Aramaic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Aramaic) name was common among [Judean](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Judea) Jews at the time of Jesus's birth,[^cite_note-45] although by that period it had been shortened to יֵשׁוּעַ (*Yeshua*) from יְהוֹשֻׁעַ‎ (*Yehoshua*);[^cite_note-46] the contraction had already occurred in later biblical books such as [Nehemiah](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Book_of_Nehemiah),[^cite_note-47] where [Joshua](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Joshua) is referred to as Yeshua.[^cite_note-48]

The name means "[God](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tetragrammaton) saves" in Hebrew, literally "[Yahweh](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Yahweh) saves",[^cite_note-49] from the [root](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hebrew_root) ישׁע (*y-š-ʿ,* 'to save') and the noun יְשׁוּעָה (*yeshuah*, 'salvation'). The [Gospel of Matthew](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gospel_of_Matthew) asserts the etymological significance of Jesus's name explicitly in the prophecy of the angel to Joseph about his birth: "you will call his name Jesus (Ἰησοῦς), for he will save (σώσει) his people from their sins".[^cite_note-50]

The fact that Moses' successor [Joshua](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Joshua) bears the same name as Jesus in the original Greek, Hebrew, and Aramaic has been given theological significance by commentators, as a parallel is often drawn between the two leaders and the etymology of their shared name ('to save'): Joshua leads the Jews into the [Promised Land](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Promised_Land), while in Christianity Jesus is understood to save both Jews and Gentiles from their sins.[^cite_note-51][^cite_note-52][^cite_note-BensonMatthew1-44][^cite_note-53]

### Jesus Christ

Since the 1st century, Christians have commonly referred to Jesus as "Jesus Christ".[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEDoninger1999212-54] The word [*Christ*](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Christ_\(title\)) is not a given name but was originally a [title or office](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Threefold_office) ("the Christ"), meaning "The [Messiah](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Messiah)".[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEPannenberg196830–31-55][^cite_note-56] The term derives from the Greek [Χριστός](https://en.wiktionary.org/wiki/%CE%A7%CF%81%CE%B9%CF%83%CF%84%CF%8C%CF%82) (*Christos*),[^cite_note-57][^cite_note-58] a [calque](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Calque) of the Hebrew word [משיח](https://en.wiktionary.org/wiki/%D7%9E%D6%B8%D7%A9%D6%B4%D7%81%D7%99%D7%97%D6%B7) (*[mashiakh](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Messiah_in_Judaism)*), transliterated into English as *messiah*.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEVine1940274–275-59] The Hebrew term means "[anointed](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Anointed)", from the verb מָשַׁח (*mashaḥ*), "to rub with oil, to anoint".[^cite_note-60] In the [Septuagint](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Septuagint), the Hebrew word was rendered into Greek as χριστός (*christos*), meaning "anointed",[^cite_note-61] from the verb χρίω (*chrio*), "to rub with oil, to anoint".[^cite_note-62] In biblical Judaism, [sacred oil](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Holy_anointing_oil) was used to anoint certain exceptionally holy people and objects as part of their religious investiture.[^cite_note-63]

Early Christians designated Jesus as "the Christ" because they believed him to be the Messiah whose arrival is [prophesied](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jesus_and_messianic_prophecy) in the [Hebrew Bible](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hebrew_Bible) (Old Testament). In post-biblical usage, *Christ* came to be viewed as a name—one part of "Jesus Christ". The term *[Christian](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Christian)*, meaning a follower of Christ, has been in use since the 1st century.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEMillsBullard1998\[httpsbooksgooglecombooksidgoq0VWw9rGICpgPA142_142\]-64]

### Canonical gospels

Main articles: [Gospel](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gospel), [Gospel harmony](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gospel_harmony), and [Historical reliability of the Gospels](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Historical_reliability_of_the_Gospels)

A four-page papyrus manuscript, which is torn in many places

A 3rd-century Greek [papyrus](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Papyrus) of the [Gospel of Luke](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gospel_of_Luke)

The four [canonical gospels](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Canonical_gospel) ([Matthew](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gospel_of_Matthew), [Mark](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gospel_of_Mark), [Luke](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gospel_of_Luke), and [John](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gospel_of_John)) are the foremost sources for the life and message of Jesus.[^cite_note-Britannica-40] Other parts of the New Testament also include references to key episodes in his life, such as the [Last Supper](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Last_Supper) in [1 Corinthians 11](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1_Corinthians_11)\:23–26.[^cite_note-65][^cite_note-FOOTNOTEBlomberg2009441–442-66][^cite_note-Fahlbusch52-67][^cite_note-FOOTNOTEEvans2003465–477-68] [Acts of the Apostles](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Acts_of_the_Apostles)[^cite_note-69] refers to Jesus's early ministry and its anticipation by [John the Baptist](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/John_the_Baptist).[^cite_note-70][^cite_note-FOOTNOTERausch200377-71][^cite_note-FOOTNOTEVermes198120,_26,_27,_29-18] Acts 1\:1–11[^cite_note-72] provides more detail about the [Ascension of Jesus](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ascension_of_Jesus)[^cite_note-73] than the canonical gospels do.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEEvans2003521–530-74] In the [undisputed Pauline letters](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pauline_epistles#Authenticity), which were written earlier than the gospels, Jesus's words or instructions are cited several times.[^cite_note-75][^cite_note-78]

Some [early Christian](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Early_Christian) groups had separate descriptions of Jesus's life and teachings that are not included in the New Testament. These include the [Gospel of Thomas](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gospel_of_Thomas), [Gospel of Peter](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gospel_of_Peter), [Gospel of Judas](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gospel_of_Judas), the [Apocryphon of James](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Apocryphon_of_James), and [many other apocryphal writings](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/New_Testament_apocrypha). Most scholars conclude that these texts were written later and are less historically reliable than the canonical gospels.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEBrown1997835–840-79][^cite_note-80][^cite_note-FOOTNOTEKeener200956-81]

#### Authorship, date, and reliability

The canonical gospels are four accounts, each attributed to a different author. The authors of the gospels are generally regarded as pseudonymous and are attributed by tradition to the [four evangelists](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Four_evangelists), each associated with Jesus or his close followers:[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEFunkHooverThe_Jesus_Seminar19933-82] Mark by [John Mark](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/John_Mark), an associate of [Peter](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Saint_Peter);[^cite_note-May_Metzger_Mark-83] [Matthew](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Matthew_the_Apostle) to one of Jesus's [disciples](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Disciple_\(Christianity\));[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEFunkHooverThe_Jesus_Seminar19933-82] [Luke](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Luke_the_Evangelist) to a companion of [Paul](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Paul_the_Apostle) mentioned in a few epistles;[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEFunkHooverThe_Jesus_Seminar19933-82] and John to another of Jesus's disciples,[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEFunkHooverThe_Jesus_Seminar19933-82] the "[beloved disciple](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Beloved_disciple)".[^cite_note-FOOTNOTECrossLivingstone2005John,_St.-84]

According to [Marcan priority](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Marcan_priority), the first to be written was the [Gospel of Mark](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gospel_of_Mark) (AD 60–75), followed by the Gospel of Matthew (AD 65–85), the Gospel of Luke (AD 65–95), and the Gospel of John (AD 75–100).[^cite_note-85] Most scholars agree that the authors of Matthew and Luke used Mark as a source for their gospels. Since Matthew and Luke also share some content not found in Mark, many scholars infer that they used a hypothetical [Q source](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Q_source) in addition to Mark, while a growing number support the [Farrer hypothesis](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Farrer_hypothesis) or [Matthean Posteriority](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Matthean_Posteriority_hypothesis), in which Matthew and Luke used each other directly.[^cite_note-\:022-86][^cite_note-TSP20222-87][^cite_note-FOOTNOTELicona2010210–221-88] Luke and Matthew treat their sources more conservatively than other ancient historians like [Diodorus Siculus](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Diodorus_Siculus), though the parallels and variations of the Synoptic gospels are typical of ancient historical biographies.[^cite_note-Kloppenborg_49–79-89][^cite_note-Licona_2016_XIII-XIV-90]

One important aspect of the study of the gospels is the [literary genre](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Literary_genre) under which they fall.[^cite_note-91] Whether the gospel authors set out to write novels, myths, histories, or biographies has a significant impact on how their works ought to be interpreted.[^cite_note-92][^cite_note-93][^cite_note-94] Although not without critics,[^cite_note-95] the view that the gospels are a type of ancient biography represents the consensus among scholars today.[^cite_note-96][^cite_note-97]

Concerning the accuracy of the accounts, viewpoints range from considering them [inerrant](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Inerrant) descriptions of Jesus's life,[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEGrudem199490–91-98] to doubting their historical reliability on various points,[^cite_note-FOOTNOTESanders19933-99] to regarding them as providing very little historical information about his life beyond the basics.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEKöstenbergerKellumQuarles2009117–125-100][^cite_note-FOOTNOTEEhrman199922–23-101]
