Matthew, Mark, and Luke are known as the Synoptic Gospels, from the Greek σύν (*syn*, 'together') and ὄψις (*opsis*, 'view'),[^cite_note-Haffner-2008-102][^cite_note-Scroggie-1995-103][^cite_note-104] because they are similar in content, narrative arrangement, language, and paragraph structure, and can readily be set side by side for synoptic comparison.[^cite_note-Haffner-2008-102][^cite_note-Scroggie-1995-103][^cite_note-105] Scholars generally agree that it is impossible to find any direct literary relationship between the Synoptic Gospels and the Gospel of John.[^cite_note-106] Many events—such as Jesus's baptism, crucifixion, and interactions with his [apostles](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Apostles_in_the_New_Testament)—appear in the Synoptic Gospels, but incidents such as the [transfiguration](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Transfiguration_of_Jesus) and Jesus's [exorcising](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Exorcism) demons[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEWitherington1997113-107] do not appear in John, which also differs on other matters, such as the [cleansing of the Temple](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cleansing_of_the_Temple).[^cite_note-108]

The Synoptics emphasize different aspects of Jesus. In Mark, Jesus is the [Son of God](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Son_of_God_\(Christianity\)) whose mighty works demonstrate the presence of [God's Kingdom](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/God's_Kingdom).[^cite_note-May_Metzger_Mark-83] He is portrayed as a tireless wonder worker and the servant of both God and humanity.[^cite_note-ThompsonPortraits-109] This short gospel records relatively few of Jesus's words or extended teachings.[^cite_note-May_Metzger_Mark-83] The Gospel of Matthew emphasizes that Jesus is the fulfilment of God's will as revealed in the Old Testament and the Lord of the Church.[^cite_note-110] He is presented as the "[Son of David](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Davidic_line)", a "king", and the Messiah.[^cite_note-ThompsonPortraits-109][^cite_note-FOOTNOTEMcGrath20064–6-111] Luke presents Jesus as the divine-human saviour who shows compassion to the needy.[^cite_note-May_Metzger_Luke-112] He is depicted as the friend of sinners and outcasts, who came to seek and save the lost.[^cite_note-ThompsonPortraits-109] This gospel includes well-known parables, such as the [Good Samaritan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Good_Samaritan) and the [Prodigal Son](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Prodigal_Son).[^cite_note-May_Metzger_Luke-112]

The [prologue to the Gospel of John](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Prologue_to_the_Gospel_of_John) identifies Jesus as an incarnation of the divine Word ([Logos](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Logos_\(Christianity\))).[^cite_note-MayMetzgerJohn-113] As the Word, Jesus is described as eternally present with God, active in all creation, and the source of humanity's moral and spiritual nature.[^cite_note-MayMetzgerJohn-113] In this gospel, Jesus is portrayed as not only greater than any past human prophet but greater than any prophet could be: he not only speaks God's Word; he is God's Word.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEHarris1985302–310-114] In the Gospel of John, Jesus reveals his divine role publicly and is depicted as the [Bread of Life](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bread_of_Life), the [Light of the World](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Light_of_the_World), the [True Vine](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/True_Vine), and more.[^cite_note-ThompsonPortraits-109]

The authors of the New Testament generally showed little interest in establishing an absolute [chronology of Jesus](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Chronology_of_Jesus)'s life or in synchronizing the episodes of his life with the secular history of the age.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTERahner2004730–731-115] As stated in [John 21\:25](https://en.wikisource.org/wiki/Bible%20\(American%20Standard\)/John#21\:25), the gospels do not claim to provide an exhaustive list of the events of Jesus's life.[^cite_note-116] The accounts were primarily written as theological documents in the context of [early Christianity](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Early_Christianity), with timelines as a secondary consideration.[^cite_note-Wiarda75-117] The gospels devote about one third of their text to the last week of Jesus's life in [Jerusalem](16043.md), referred to as the [Passion](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Passion_\(Christianity\)).[^cite_note-Turner613-118] They do not provide enough detail to satisfy the demands of modern historians regarding exact dates, but it is possible to draw from them a general picture of Jesus's life story.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTESanders19933-99][^cite_note-FOOTNOTERahner2004730–731-115][^cite_note-Wiarda75-117]

### Genealogy and nativity

Main articles: [Genealogy of Jesus](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Genealogy_of_Jesus) and [Nativity of Jesus](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nativity_of_Jesus)

Jesus was Jewish,[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEVermes198120,_26,_27,_29-18] born to [Mary](73513.md), the wife of [Joseph](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Saint_Joseph).[^cite_note-119] The Gospels of Matthew and Luke offer two different accounts of his [genealogy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Genealogy). Matthew traces Jesus's ancestry to [Abraham](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Abraham) through [David](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/David),[^cite_note-120][^cite_note-FOOTNOTESanders199380–91-121] while Luke traces Jesus's ancestry through [Adam](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Adam) to God.[^cite_note-122][^cite_note-FOOTNOTEBrown1978\[httpsbooksgooglecombooksidML1mnUBwmhcCpgPA163_163\]-123] The lists are identical between Abraham and David but differ markedly from that point onward; Matthew has 27 generations from David to Joseph, whereas Luke has 42, with almost no overlap between the names on the two lists.[^cite_note-124][^cite_note-125] Various theories have been put forward to explain why the two genealogies are so different.[^cite_note-126]

A Nativity scene; men and animals surround Mary and newborn Jesus, who are covered in light

*Adoration of the Shepherds* by [Gerard van Honthorst](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gerard_van_Honthorst), 1622

Both Matthew and Luke describe Jesus's birth, particularly that he was born to a virgin named Mary in [Bethlehem](4312.md) in fulfilment of [prophecy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Prophecy). Luke's account emphasizes events before the [birth of Jesus](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Birth_of_Jesus) and centres on Mary, while Matthew's mostly covers events after the birth and centres on Joseph.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEMillsBullard1998556-127][^cite_note-marsh37-128][^cite_note-FOOTNOTEMorris199226-129] Both accounts state that Mary was engaged to a man named Joseph, who was descended from King David and was not Jesus's biological father, and both support the doctrine of the [virgin birth of Jesus](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Virgin_birth_of_Jesus), according to which Jesus was miraculously conceived by the [Holy Spirit](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Holy_Spirit) in Mary's womb when she was still a virgin.[^cite_note-Jeffrey-130][^cite_note-FOOTNOTECoxEasley200730–37-131][^cite_note-132] At the same time, there is evidence, at least in the Lukan *Acts of the Apostles*, that Jesus was thought to have had, like many figures in antiquity, a dual paternity, since there it is stated that he descended from the seed or loins of David.[^cite_note-133] By [taking Jesus as his own son](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Adoption_in_Judaism), Joseph is understood to confer on him the necessary Davidic descent.[^cite_note-134] Some scholars suggest that Jesus had [Levite](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Levite) heritage from Mary, based on her blood relationship with [Elizabeth](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Elizabeth_\(biblical_figure\)).[^cite_note-135]

74.9 × 102.2 cm

*The Circumcision* by [Giovanni Bellini](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Giovanni_Bellini), c. 1500. The work depicts the [circumcision of Jesus](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Circumcision_of_Jesus).

In Matthew, Joseph is troubled because Mary, his betrothed, is [pregnant](1771587.md),[^cite_note-136] but in the first of [Joseph's four dreams](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/St._Joseph's_dream) an angel assures him not to be afraid to take Mary as his wife because her child was conceived by the Holy Spirit.[^cite_note-Talbert-137] In [Matthew 2\:1](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Matthew_2\:1)–[12](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Matthew_2\:12), [wise men](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Biblical_Magi) or [Magi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Magi) from the [East](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/East) bring gifts to the young Jesus as the [King of the Jews](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jesus,_King_of_the_Jews). They find him in a house in Bethlehem. [Herod the Great](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Herod_the_Great) hears of Jesus's birth and, wanting him killed, [orders the killings of male infants](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Massacre_of_the_Innocents) in Bethlehem and its surroundings. However, an angel warns Joseph in his second dream, and the family [flees to Egypt](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Flight_to_Egypt), later returning and settling in Nazareth.[^cite_note-Talbert-137][^cite_note-FOOTNOTEHarris1985272–285-138][^cite_note-139]

In Luke 1\:31–38, Mary learns from the angel [Gabriel](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gabriel) that she will conceive and bear a child called Jesus through the action of the Holy Spirit.[^cite_note-marsh37-128][^cite_note-Jeffrey-130] When Mary is due to give birth, she and Joseph travel from Nazareth to Joseph's ancestral home in Bethlehem to register in the census ordered by [Caesar Augustus](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Caesar_Augustus). While there, Mary gives birth to Jesus, and, as they have found no room in the inn, she places the newborn in a [manger](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Manger).[^cite_note-140] An [angel announces the birth to a group of shepherds](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Annunciation_to_the_shepherds), who go to Bethlehem to see Jesus and subsequently spread the news abroad.[^cite_note-141] Luke 2\:21 recounts how Joseph and Mary [have their baby circumcised on the eighth day after birth](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Circumcision_of_Jesus) and name him Jesus, as Gabriel had commanded Mary.[^cite_note-142] After the [presentation of Jesus at the Temple](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Presentation_of_Jesus_at_the_Temple), Joseph, Mary, and Jesus return to Nazareth.[^cite_note-marsh37-128][^cite_note-Jeffrey-130]

### Early life, family, and profession

Main articles: [Christ Child](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Christ_Child) and [Holy Family](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Holy_Family)

See also: [Return of the family of Jesus to Nazareth](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Return_of_the_family_of_Jesus_to_Nazareth), [Unknown years of Jesus](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Unknown_years_of_Jesus), and [Brothers of Jesus](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Brothers_of_Jesus)

Mary and Joseph find Jesus in the Temple

*[The Finding of the Saviour in the Temple](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/The_Finding_of_the_Saviour_in_the_Temple)* by [William Holman Hunt](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/William_Holman_Hunt), 1860

Jesus's childhood home is identified in the Gospels of Luke and Matthew as Nazareth, a town in [Galilee](12639.md) in present-day [Israel](9282173.md), where he lived with his family. Although Joseph appears in descriptions of Jesus's childhood, no mention is made of him thereafter.[^cite_note-143][^cite_note-144] His other family members, including his mother Mary; [his four brothers](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Brothers_of_Jesus), [James](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/James,_brother_of_Jesus), [Joses (or Joseph)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Joses,_brother_of_Jesus), [Judas](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jude,_brother_of_Jesus), and [Simon](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Simon,_brother_of_Jesus); and his unnamed sisters, are mentioned in the Gospels and other sources.[^cite_note-145] Jesus's maternal grandparents are named [Joachim](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Joachim) and [Anne](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Saint_Anne) in the [Gospel of James](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gospel_of_James).[^cite_note-146] The Gospel of Luke records that Mary was a relative of Elizabeth, the mother of John the Baptist.[^cite_note-147] Some extra-biblical contemporary sources consider Jesus and John the Baptist to be second cousins, based on the belief that Elizabeth was the daughter of [Sobe](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sobe_\(sister_of_Saint_Anne\)), the sister of Anne.[^cite_note-148][^cite_note-149][^cite_note-150]

The Gospel of Mark reports that at the beginning of [his ministry](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ministry_of_Jesus), Jesus comes into conflict with his neighbours and family.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEHarris1985270–272-151] Jesus's mother and brothers come to get him[^cite_note-152] because people are saying that [he is out of his mind](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mental_health_of_Jesus).[^cite_note-153] Jesus responds that his followers are his true family. In the Gospel of John, Jesus and his mother attend a [wedding at Cana](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Wedding_at_Cana), where he performs his first miracle at her request.[^cite_note-154] Later, she is present at his crucifixion, and he expresses concern for her well-being.[^cite_note-155]

Jesus is called a τέκτων (*[tektōn](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tekt%C5%8Dn)*) in [Mark 6\:3](https://en.wikisource.org/wiki/Bible%20\(American%20Standard\)/Mark#6\:3), a term traditionally understood as "carpenter" but which can also refer to makers of objects in various materials, including builders.[^cite_note-156][^cite_note-FOOTNOTEDickson200868–69-157] Given the term's broad semantic range and "the socio-historical reality of a common Nazarene τέκτων", Matthew K. Robinson, a minister and academic, prefers to translate τέκτων as 'builder-craftsman'.[^cite_note-158][^cite_note-159] The Gospels indicate that Jesus could read, paraphrase, and debate scripture, but this does not necessarily mean that he received formal scribal training.[^cite_note-160]

The Gospel of Luke reports two journeys of Jesus and his parents in Jerusalem during his childhood. They come to the [Temple in Jerusalem](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Temple_in_Jerusalem) for the [presentation of Jesus](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Presentation_of_Jesus) as a baby in accordance with Jewish law, where a man named [Simeon](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Simeon_\(Gospel_of_Luke\)) prophesies about Jesus and Mary.[^cite_note-161] When Jesus, at the age of twelve, goes missing on a pilgrimage to Jerusalem for [Passover](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Passover), his parents [find him in the Temple](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Finding_in_the_Temple) sitting among the teachers, listening to them and asking questions, and the people are amazed at his understanding and answers. Mary scolds Jesus for going missing, to which Jesus replies that he must "be in his Father's house".[^cite_note-162]

### Baptism and temptation

Main articles: [Baptism of Jesus](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Baptism_of_Jesus) and [Temptation of Christ](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Temptation_of_Christ)

Jesus is baptized by John. The Holy Spirit in the form of a dove is overhead.

*[The Baptism of Christ](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Baptism_of_Jesus) by [John the Baptist](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/John_the_Baptist)*, by [Almeida Júnior](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Almeida_J%C3%BAnior), 1895

The [synoptic gospels](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Synoptic_gospels) describe [Jesus's baptism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Baptism_of_Jesus) in the [Jordan River](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jordan_River) and [the temptations he faced](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Temptation_of_Christ) while spending forty days in the [Judaean Desert](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Judaean_Desert) as a preparation for his public ministry.[^cite_note-163] In each of these accounts, the accounts of Jesus's baptism is preceded by information about John the Baptist.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEBlomberg2009224–229-164][^cite_note-FOOTNOTEKöstenbergerKellumQuarles2009141–143-165][^cite_note-FOOTNOTEMcGrath200616–22-166] They portray John preaching repentance for the forgiveness of sins, encouraging the giving of [alms](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Alms) to the poor,[^cite_note-167] baptizing people in the region of the Jordan River around [Perea](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Perea), and foretelling the arrival of someone "more powerful" than he.[^cite_note-168][^cite_note-169]



Jesus and the Devil depicted in *[The Temptation of Christ](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Temptation_of_Christ)*, by [Ary Scheffer](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ary_Scheffer), 1854

In the Gospel of Mark, John the Baptist baptizes Jesus, and as Jesus comes up out of the water he sees the [Holy Spirit](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Holy_Spirit) descending on him like a dove, and a voice comes from heaven and declares him to be God's Son.[^cite_note-170] This is one of two events described in the Gospels where a voice from Heaven refers to Jesus as "Son", the other being the [Transfiguration](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Transfiguration_of_Jesus).[^cite_note-FOOTNOTELee200421–30-171][^cite_note-Nobbs-172] The Spirit then drives him into the wilderness, where he is tempted by [Satan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Satan).[^cite_note-173] After John's arrest, Jesus begins his ministry in Galilee.[^cite_note-174]

In the Gospel of Matthew, when Jesus comes to John to be baptized, John protests, saying, "I need to be baptized by you."[^cite_note-175] Jesus instructs him to proceed with the baptism "to fulfil all righteousness".[^cite_note-176] Matthew then narrates three specific temptations that Satan offers Jesus in the wilderness.[^cite_note-177] In the Gospel of Luke, the Holy Spirit descends in bodily form like a dove after all the people have been baptized and while Jesus is [praying](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Prayers_of_Jesus).[^cite_note-178] Later, John implicitly acknowledges Jesus by sending his followers to inquire about him.[^cite_note-179] Luke also describes three temptations experienced by Jesus in the wilderness before he begins his ministry in Galilee.[^cite_note-180]

The Gospel of John does not narrate Jesus's baptism and temptation.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTECrossLivingstone2005Jesus_Christ-181] Instead, John the Baptist testifies that he saw the Spirit descend and remain on Jesus.[^cite_note-182][^cite_note-FOOTNOTEBoringCraddock2004\[httpsbooksgooglecombooksidN0tLXRIiIe0CpgPA292_292\]-183] John publicly proclaims Jesus as the [Lamb of God](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lamb_of_God), and some of John's followers become disciples of Jesus.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTETheissenMerz199817–62-184] Before John is imprisoned, Jesus leads his followers to baptize,[^cite_note-185] and they baptize more people than John.[^cite_note-186]

### &#x20;Public ministry

Main article: [Ministry of Jesus](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ministry_of_Jesus)

Jesus sits atop a mount, preaching to a crowd

*Sermon on the Mount*, by [Carl Bloch](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Carl_Bloch), 1877, depicts [Jesus's important discourse](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sermon_on_the_Mount).

The Synoptics depict two main geographical settings in Jesus's ministry. The first takes place in Galilee, north of [Judea](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Judea), where Jesus conducts a largely successful ministry; the second occurs in Jerusalem, where he is rejected and killed.[^cite_note-ISBEO-187] Often referred to as "[rabbi](51273.md)",[^cite_note-ISBEO-187] Jesus delivers his message orally.[^cite_note-Dunn2013-188] In these accounts, he forbids those who recognize him as the messiah—including people he heals and demons he is said to exorcise—to speak about it (see [Messianic Secret](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Messianic_Secret)).[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEHarris1985285–296-189] By contrast, the Gospel of John portrays Jesus's ministry as taking place primarily in and around Jerusalem rather than in Galilee, and his divine nature is more openly proclaimed and recognized.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEHarris1985302–310-114]

Scholars commonly divide the ministry of Jesus into several stages. The Galilean ministry begins when Jesus returns to Galilee from the [Judaean Desert](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Judaean_Desert) after resisting the temptations of [Satan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Satan). He then preaches throughout Galilee, and in [Matthew 4\:18–20](https://en.wikisource.org/wiki/Bible%20\(American%20Standard\)/Matthew#4\:18), his [first disciples](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/First_disciples_of_Jesus)—who will later form the core of the early Church—encounter him and begin to follow him.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEMcGrath200616–22-166][^cite_note-FOOTNOTERedford2007117–130-190] This period includes the [Sermon on the Mount](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sermon_on_the_Mount), one of Jesus's major discourses,[^cite_note-FOOTNOTERedford2007117–130-190][^cite_note-191] as well as the [calming of the storm](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Calming_of_the_storm), the [feeding of the 5,000](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Feeding_of_the_5,000), [walking on water](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jesus_walking_on_water), and various other miracles and [parables](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Parables_of_Jesus).[^cite_note-FOOTNOTERedford2007143–160-192] It concludes with the [Confession of Peter](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Confession_of_Peter) and the Transfiguration.[^cite_note-193][^cite_note-Barton132-194]

As Jesus travels towards Jerusalem, during what is often called the Perean ministry, he returns to the region where he was baptized, roughly a third of the way down from the [Sea of Galilee](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sea_of_Galilee) along the Jordan River.[^cite_note-195][^cite_note-FOOTNOTECoxEasley2007137-196][^cite_note-FOOTNOTERedford2007211–229-197] The [final phase of his ministry](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ministry_of_Jesus#Final_ministry_in_Jerusalem), In Jerusalem, begins with his [triumphal entry](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Triumphal_entry_into_Jerusalem) into the city on [Palm Sunday](24178.md).[^cite_note-FOOTNOTECoxEasley2007155–170-198] In the Synoptic Gospels, during that week Jesus [drives the money changers](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cleansing_of_the_Temple) from the [Second Temple](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Second_Temple), and [Judas bargains to betray](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bargain_of_Judas) him. This period culminates in the [Last Supper](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Last_Supper) and, in the Johannine account, the [Farewell Discourse](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Farewell_Discourse).[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEBlomberg2009224–229-164][^cite_note-FOOTNOTECoxEasley2007155–170-198][^cite_note-FOOTNOTERedford2007257–274-199]

#### Disciples and followers

Main articles: [Apostles in the New Testament](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Apostles_in_the_New_Testament) and [Commissioning of the Twelve Apostles](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Commissioning_of_the_Twelve_Apostles)



*The Exhortation to the Apostles*, by [James Tissot](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/James_Tissot), portrays Jesus talking to his twelve disciples.

Near the beginning of his ministry, Jesus [appoints twelve apostles](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Commissioning_of_the_Twelve_Apostles). In Matthew and Mark, Jesus calls his first four apostles, who are fishermen, and they are described as immediately leaving their nets to follow him.[^cite_note-200] In John, Jesus's first two apostles are initially disciples of John the Baptist; the Baptist sees Jesus and calls him the Lamb of God, and the two, hearing this, begin to follow Jesus.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEBrown198825–27-201][^cite_note-FOOTNOTEBoringCraddock2004292–293-202] In addition to the Twelve Apostles, the introduction of the [Sermon on the Plain](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sermon_on_the_Plain) in Luke identifies a much larger group of people as disciples.[^cite_note-203] In [Luke 10\:1–16](https://en.wikisource.org/wiki/Bible%20\(American%20Standard\)/Luke#10\:1), Jesus sends [70 or 72 of his followers](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Seventy_disciples) out in pairs to prepare towns for his prospective visits; they are instructed to accept hospitality, heal the sick, and proclaim the [Kingdom of God](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kingdom_of_God).[^cite_note-204]

#### &#x20;Teachings and miracles

Main articles: [Sermon on the Mount](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sermon_on_the_Mount), [Parables of Jesus](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Parables_of_Jesus), and [Miracles of Jesus](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Miracles_of_Jesus)

See also: [Sermon on the Plain](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sermon_on_the_Plain), [Five Discourses of Matthew](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Five_Discourses_of_Matthew), [Farewell Discourse](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Farewell_Discourse), [Olivet Discourse](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Olivet_Discourse), and [Bread of Life Discourse](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bread_of_Life_Discourse)



*[Jesus and the rich young man](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jesus_and_the_rich_young_man)* by [Heinrich Hofmann](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Heinrich_Hofmann_\(painter\)), 1889

In the Synoptics, Jesus teaches extensively—often in [parables](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Parable)[^cite_note-FOOTNOTETheissenMerz1998316–346-205]—about the Kingdom of God. Jesus also speaks of the "[Son of Man](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Son_of_man_\(Christianity\))", an [apocalyptic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Apocalypse) figure who will come to gather the chosen.[^cite_note-Britannica-40] Jesus calls people to repent of their sins and to devote themselves wholly to God.[^cite_note-Britannica-40] He instructs his followers to observe [Jewish law](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jewish_law), although he is perceived by some contemporaries as having broken the law himself, for example in relation to [Sabbath observance](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sabbath).[^cite_note-Britannica-40] When asked what the [greatest commandment](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Great_Commandment) is, Jesus replies: "You shall love the Lord your God with all your heart, and with all your soul, and with all your mind ... And a second is like it: 'You shall love your neighbour as yourself.'"[^cite_note-206] Other ethical teachings attributed to Jesus include [loving one's enemies](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Matthew_5\:44), refraining from [hatred](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hatred) and [lust](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lust), [turning the other cheek](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Turning_the_other_cheek), and [forgiving](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Forgiveness) those who have sinned against oneself.[^cite_note-207][^cite_note-208]

The Gospel of John presents the teachings of Jesus not merely as his own preaching but as divine [revelation](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Revelation). John the Baptist, for example, states in [John 3\:34](https://en.wikisource.org/wiki/Bible%20\(American%20Standard\)/John#3\:34): "He whom God has sent speaks the words of God, for he gives the Spirit without measure." In [John 7\:16](https://en.wikisource.org/wiki/Bible%20\(American%20Standard\)/John#7\:16), Jesus says, "My teaching is not mine but his who sent me." He reiterates this in [John 14\:10](https://en.wikisource.org/wiki/Bible%20\(American%20Standard\)/John#14\:10): "Do you not believe that I am in the Father and the Father is in me? The words that I say to you I do not speak on my own; but the Father who dwells in me does his works."[^cite_note-Osborn-1993-209][^cite_note-210]



*[The Return of the Prodigal Son](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Parable_of_the_Prodigal_Son)* by [Pompeo Batoni](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pompeo_Batoni), 1773

Approximately 30 parables constitute about one-third of Jesus's recorded teachings.[^cite_note-Osborn-1993-209][^cite_note-211] The parables appear both within longer sermons and at various other places in the narrative.[^cite_note-212] They often contain symbolism and typically relate aspects of the physical world to [spiritual](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Spirituality) realities.[^cite_note-213][^cite_note-214] Common themes include the kindness and generosity of God, as well as the dangers and consequences of transgression.[^cite_note-215] Some parables, such as that of the [Prodigal Son](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Prodigal_Son),[^cite_note-216] are relatively straightforward, while others, such as the [Growing Seed](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Parable_of_the_Growing_Seed),[^cite_note-217] are more complex, profound, and difficult to interpret.[^cite_note-218] When his disciples ask why he speaks to the people in parables, Jesus replies that the chosen disciples have been granted "to know the secrets of the kingdom of heaven", unlike the rest, adding: "For the one who has will be given more and he will have in abundance. But the one who does not have will be deprived even more", and he goes on to say that most of their generation have developed "dull hearts" and are therefore unable to understand.[^cite_note-219]

Jesus, his head surrounded by a halo, puts his hands on a leper, thereby healing him

[Jesus cleansing a leper](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jesus_cleansing_a_leper), mediaeval [mosaic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mosaic) from the [Monreale Cathedral](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Monreale_Cathedral), late 12th to mid-13th centuries

In the gospel accounts, Jesus devotes a substantial portion of his ministry to performing [miracles](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Miracle), especially healings.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEGreenMcKnightMarshall1992299-220] These miracles are commonly classified into two main categories: healing miracles and nature miracles.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTETwelftree1999350-221][^cite_note-FOOTNOTELevine20064-222][^cite_note-223] The healing miracles include cures of physical ailments, [exorcisms](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Exorcism),[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEWitherington1997113-107][^cite_note-FOOTNOTETheissenMerz1998298-224] and [the raising of the dead](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Miracles_of_Jesus#Resurrection_of_the_dead).[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEGreenMcKnightMarshall1992300-225][^cite_note-Oxford_Companion-226][^cite_note-227][^cite_note-228] The nature miracles demonstrate authority over the natural world and include [turning water into wine](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Turning_water_into_wine), walking on water, and calming a storm, among others. Jesus attributes his miracles to a divine source. When opponents accuse him of casting out demons by the power of [Beelzebub](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Beelzebub), the prince of demons, he replies that he does so by the "Spirit of God" ([Matthew 12\:28](https://en.wikisource.org/wiki/Bible%20\(American%20Standard\)/Matthew#12\:28)) or "finger of God", arguing that it would be illogical for Satan to undermine his own domain; he also asks, if he exorcises by Beelzebub, "by whom do your sons cast them out?"[^cite_note-229][^cite_note-Britannica-40][^cite_note-230] In Matthew 12\:31–32, he further states that while all kinds of sin, including "insults against God" or "insults against the Son of Man", may be forgiven, blasphemy against "The [Holy Spirit](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Holy_Spirit)" will never be forgiven, and those guilty of it bear their sin permanently.[^cite_note-231]

In John, Jesus's miracles are described as "signs", performed to manifest his mission and identity.[^cite_note-Sign-232][^cite_note-FOOTNOTEEhrman2009\[httpsarchiveorgdetailsjesusinterrupted00ehrm_0page84_84\]-233] In the Synoptic Gospels, when some teachers of the law and Pharisees ask him for a miraculous sign to validate his authority, Jesus refuses,[^cite_note-Sign-232] saying that no sign will be given to a corrupt and evil generation except the sign of the prophet [Jonah](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jonah). In the Synoptics, the crowds typically respond to his miracles with awe and press upon him to heal their sick, whereas in John, Jesus is depicted as less constrained by the crowds, who often respond to his signs with belief and trust.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTETwelftree1999236-234] A feature common to all the miracle narratives is that Jesus performs them freely and does not request or accept payment.[^cite_note-235] The miracle stories are frequently interwoven with teachings, and the miracles themselves often carry a [didactic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Didactic_method) dimension.[^cite_note-236][^cite_note-FOOTNOTETwelftree199995-237] Many emphasize the importance of faith: in the [cleansing of ten lepers](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cleansing_ten_lepers) and the [raising of Jairus's daughter](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Raising_of_Jairus's_daughter), for instance, the beneficiaries are told that their healing is due to their faith.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEDonahueHarrington2002182-238][^cite_note-239]

In *A Marginal Jew*, scholar [John P. Meier](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/John_P._Meier) argues that "the miracle traditions about Jesus' public ministry are already so widely attested in various sources" that any "total fabrication by the early church is, practically speaking, impossible". He bases this claim on literary sources such as the Gospels of Matthew, Luke, and John, as well as on the writings of the historian Josephus. Meier contends that the "criterion of multiple attestation of sources and forms" supports the conclusion that Jesus performed "extraordinary deeds" which his contemporaries regarded as miracles.[^cite_note-240]

Scholar [Paul J. Achtemeier](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Paul_J._Achtemeier) argues that such miracles were not unique to Jesus in the ancient world and were perceived as ambiguous even by eyewitnesses. He notes that Jesus likely performed acts understood as exorcisms, which were "accepted as reality by his contemporaries", but that these should not be seen as having "probative value with respect to Jesus," since witnesses could claim that he was working with either Satan or God.[^cite_note-241] Scholar [Gregory Sterling](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gregory_Sterling) observes that, in the case of Jesus's alleged exorcisms, "For first-century Galileans who believed in the personal presence of evil in the form of demons, Jesus' act was a validation of his ministry."[^cite_note-242]

#### Proclamation, Transfiguration, and Passion Week

Main articles: [Confession of Peter](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Confession_of_Peter) and [Transfiguration of Jesus](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Transfiguration_of_Jesus)



The [Transfiguration of Jesus](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Transfiguration_of_Jesus), depicted by [Carl Bloch](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Carl_Bloch), 19th century

At approximately the midpoint of each of the three Synoptic Gospels, two significant events are narrated: the Confession of [Peter](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Saint_Peter) and the Transfiguration of Jesus—not mentioned in the Gospel of John.[^cite_note-Barton132-194][^cite_note-243][^cite_note-FOOTNOTELee200421–30-171][^cite_note-Nobbs-172][^cite_note-FOOTNOTECrossLivingstone2005John,_Gospel_of-244] In the Confession of Peter, Peter declares to Jesus, "You are the Messiah, the Son of the living God";[^cite_note-245][^cite_note-246][^cite_note-FOOTNOTEDonahueHarrington2002336-247] Jesus affirms that this is a divinely revealed truth.[^cite_note-248][^cite_note-FOOTNOTEPannenberg196853–54-249] Following this confession, Jesus begins to tell his disciples about his forthcoming suffering, death, and resurrection.[^cite_note-250] In the Transfiguration,[^cite_note-SynopticTransfiguration-251][^cite_note-FOOTNOTELee200421–30-171][^cite_note-Nobbs-172][^cite_note-Barton132-194] Jesus takes Peter and two other apostles up an unnamed mountain, where "he was transfigured before them, and his face shone like the sun, and his clothes became dazzling white".[^cite_note-FOOTNOTELee200472–76-252] A bright cloud envelops them, and a voice from the cloud proclaims, "This is my Son, the Beloved; with him I am well pleased; listen to him."[^cite_note-SynopticTransfiguration-251][^cite_note-FOOTNOTELee200421–30-171]

The description of the final week of Jesus's life—often referred to as [Passion Week](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Passion_Week)—occupies roughly one-third of the narrative in the canonical gospels.[^cite_note-Turner613-118] This section begins with Jesus's [triumphal entry into Jerusalem](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Triumphal_entry_into_Jerusalem) and concludes with his crucifixion.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEBlomberg2009224–229-164][^cite_note-FOOTNOTECoxEasley2007155–170-198]

#### Activities in Jerusalem

Main articles: [Triumphal entry into Jerusalem](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Triumphal_entry_into_Jerusalem), [Cleansing of the Temple](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cleansing_of_the_Temple), and [Bargain of Judas](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bargain_of_Judas)

Jesus, riding a donkey colt, rides towards Jerusalem. A large crowd greets him outside the walls.

A painting of Jesus's [final entry into Jerusalem](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Triumphal_entry_into_Jerusalem), by [Jean-Léon Gérôme](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jean-L%C3%A9on_G%C3%A9r%C3%B4me), 1897

In the Synoptic Gospels, the final week in [Jerusalem](16043.md) concludes the journey through Perea and Judea that Jesus began in Galilee.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTECoxEasley2007155–170-198] Jesus enters Jerusalem riding a young donkey, evoking the motif of [the Messiah's donkey](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/The_Messiah's_Donkey) from the [Book of Zechariah](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Book_of_Zechariah), in the humble king of the Jews comes to the city in this manner.[^cite_note-253][^cite_note-May_Metzger_Mark-83] As he proceeds, people spread cloaks and small branches of trees ([palm fronds](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Palm_branch)) on the road before him and chant lines from Psalm 118\:25–26.[^cite_note-254][^cite_note-FOOTNOTEBoringCraddock2004256–258-255][^cite_note-FOOTNOTEMajerníkPonessaManhardt2005133–134-256][^cite_note-FOOTNOTEEvans2003381–395-257]

Jesus next expels the money changers from the Temple, accusing them of turning it into a den of thieves through their commercial activities. Most scholars agree that it is overwhelmingly likely that Jesus did something in the temple and mentioned its destruction.[^cite_note-258][^cite_note-FOOTNOTEHarris1985285–296-189] In John, the Cleansing of the Temple occurs at the beginning of Jesus's ministry instead of at the end.[^cite_note-259][^cite_note-FOOTNOTEHarris1985302–310-114] Ancient compositional practices involved such chronological displacement and compression, with even reliable biographers like [Plutarch](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Plutarch) displaying them.[^cite_note-260]

Jesus comes into conflict with the Jewish elders, such as when they [question his authority](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Authority_of_Jesus_questioned) and when he [criticizes them and calls them hypocrites](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Woes_of_the_Pharisees).[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEBoringCraddock2004256–258-255][^cite_note-FOOTNOTEEvans2003381–395-257] [Judas Iscariot](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Judas_Iscariot), one of the [twelve apostles](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Twelve_apostles), secretly strikes a bargain with the Jewish elders, agreeing to betray Jesus to them for [30 silver coins](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/30_silver_coins).[^cite_note-261][^cite_note-262]

The Gospel of John recounts two other feasts in which Jesus taught in Jerusalem before the Passion Week.[^cite_note-263][^cite_note-FOOTNOTEHarris1985270–272-151] In [Bethany](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bethany), a village near Jerusalem, Jesus [raises Lazarus from the dead](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Raising_of_Lazarus). This potent sign[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEHarris1985302–310-114] increases the tension with authorities,[^cite_note-FOOTNOTECoxEasley2007155–170-198] who conspire to kill him.[^cite_note-264][^cite_note-FOOTNOTEHarris1985270–272-151] [Mary of Bethany](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mary_of_Bethany) anoints Jesus's feet, foreshadowing his entombment.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEFunkHooverThe_Jesus_Seminar1993401–470-265] Jesus then makes his messianic entry into Jerusalem.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEHarris1985270–272-151] The cheering crowds greeting Jesus as he enters Jerusalem add to the animosity between him and the establishment.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTECoxEasley2007155–170-198] In John, Jesus has already cleansed the Second Temple during an earlier Passover visit to Jerusalem. John next recounts Jesus's Last Supper with his disciples.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEHarris1985270–272-151]

#### Last Supper

Main article: [Last Supper](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Last_Supper)

See also: [Jesus predicts his betrayal](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jesus_predicts_his_betrayal), [Denial of Peter](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Denial_of_Peter), and [Last Supper in Christian art](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Last_Supper_in_Christian_art)

A depiction of the Last Supper. Jesus sits in the centre, his apostles gathered around on either side of him.

The [Last Supper](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Last_Supper), depicted by [Juan de Juanes](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Juan_de_Juanes), c. 1562

The Last Supper is the final meal that Jesus shared with his twelve apostles in Jerusalem before his crucifixion. The Last Supper is mentioned in all four canonical gospels; Paul's [First Epistle to the Corinthians](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/First_Epistle_to_the_Corinthians)[^cite_note-266] also refers to it.[^cite_note-Fahlbusch52-67][^cite_note-FOOTNOTEEvans2003465–477-68][^cite_note-FOOTNOTECoxEasley2007180–191-267] During the meal, [Jesus predicts](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jesus_predicts_his_betrayal) that one of his apostles will betray him.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTECoxEasley2007182-268] Despite each Apostle's assertion that he would not betray him, Jesus reiterates that the betrayer would be one of those present. Matthew 26\:23–25 and John 13\:26–27 identify Judas as the traitor.[^cite_note-Fahlbusch52-67][^cite_note-FOOTNOTEEvans2003465–477-68][^cite_note-FOOTNOTECoxEasley2007182-268]

In the Synoptics, Jesus takes bread, breaks it, and gives it to the disciples, saying, "This is my body, which is given for you." He then has them all drink from a cup, saying, "This cup that is poured out for you is the new covenant in my blood."[^cite_note-269][^cite_note-Fahlbusch52-67][^cite_note-FOOTNOTECrossLivingstone2005Eucharist-270] The Christian [sacrament](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sacrament) or [ordinance](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ordinance_\(Christian\)) of the [Eucharist](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Eucharist) is based on these events.[^cite_note-271] Although the Gospel of John does not include a description of the bread-and-wine ritual during the Last Supper, most scholars agree that John 6\:22–59 (the [Bread of Life Discourse](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bread_of_Life_Discourse)) has a eucharistic character and resonates with the [institution narratives](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Origin_of_the_Eucharist#Institution_narratives) in the Synoptic Gospels and in the Pauline writings on the Last Supper.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEFreedman2000792-272]

In all four gospels, Jesus predicts that Peter will deny knowledge of him three times before the [cock](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rooster) crows the next morning.[^cite_note-Denial-273][^cite_note-274] In Luke and John, the prediction is made during the Supper.[^cite_note-275] In Matthew and Mark, the prediction is made after the Supper; Jesus also predicts that all his disciples will desert him.[^cite_note-276][^cite_note-FOOTNOTEWalvoordZuck198383–85-277] The Gospel of John provides the only account of [Jesus washing his disciples' feet](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Maundy_\(foot_washing\)) after the meal.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEHarris1985272–285-138] John also includes a long sermon by Jesus, preparing his disciples (now without Judas) for his departure. Chapters 14–17 of the Gospel of John are known as the [Farewell Discourse](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Farewell_Discourse) and are a significant source of [Christological](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Christological) content.[^cite_note-278][^cite_note-279]

#### Agony in the Garden, betrayal, and arrest

Main articles: [Agony in the Garden](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Agony_in_the_Garden), [Kiss of Judas](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kiss_of_Judas), and [Arrest of Jesus](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Arrest_of_Jesus)

Judas kisses Jesus, and soldiers rush to seize the latter.

A depiction of the [kiss of Judas](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kiss_of_Judas) and [arrest of Jesus](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Arrest_of_Jesus), by [Caravaggio](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Caravaggio), c. 1602

In the Synoptics, Jesus and his disciples go to the garden [Gethsemane](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gethsemane), where Jesus prays to be spared his coming ordeal. Then Judas comes with an armed mob, sent by the chief priests, [scribes](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Scribe#Judaism) and elders. He [kisses Jesus](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kiss_of_Judas) to identify him to the crowd, which then [arrests Jesus](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Arrest_of_Jesus). In an attempt to stop them, an unnamed disciple of Jesus uses a sword to cut off the ear [of a man](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Malchus) in the crowd. After Jesus's arrest, his disciples go into hiding, and Peter, when questioned, thrice [denies](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Denial_of_Peter) knowing Jesus. After the third denial, Peter hears the cock crow and recalls Jesus's prediction about his denial. Peter then weeps bitterly.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEWalvoordZuck198383–85-277][^cite_note-FOOTNOTEHarris1985285–296-189][^cite_note-Denial-273]

In John 18\:1–11, Jesus does not pray to be spared his crucifixion, as the gospel portrays him as scarcely touched by such human weakness.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTECrossLivingstone2005Jesus-280] The people who arrest him are [Roman soldiers](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Imperial_Roman_army) and Temple guards.[^cite_note-281] Instead of being betrayed by a kiss, Jesus proclaims his identity, and when he does, the soldiers and officers fall to the ground. The gospel identifies Peter as the disciple who used the sword, and Jesus rebukes him for it.

#### Trials by the Sanhedrin, Herod, and Pilate

Main articles: [Sanhedrin trial of Jesus](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sanhedrin_trial_of_Jesus), [Pilate's Court](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pilate's_Court), and [Jesus at Herod's Court](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jesus_at_Herod's_Court)

See also: [Jesus, King of the Jews](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jesus,_King_of_the_Jews); [John 18\:38](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/John_18\:38); and [Ecce homo](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ecce_homo)

After his arrest, Jesus is taken late at night to the private residence of the high priest, [Caiaphas](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Caiaphas), who had been installed by Pilate's predecessor, the Roman procurator [Valerius Gratus](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Valerius_Gratus).[^cite_note-282] The [Sanhedrin](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sanhedrin) was a Jewish judicial body.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEBrown1997146-283] The gospel accounts differ on the [details of the trials](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sanhedrin_trial_of_Jesus).[^cite_note-284] In Matthew 26\:57, Mark 14\:53, and Luke 22\:54, Jesus is taken to the house of the high priest, Caiaphas, where he is [mocked](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mocking_of_Jesus) and beaten that night. Early the next morning, the chief priests and scribes lead Jesus away into their council.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEEvans2003487–500-285][^cite_note-FOOTNOTEBlomberg2009396–400-286][^cite_note-Holman608-287] John 18\:12–14 states that Jesus is first taken to [Annas](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Annas), Caiaphas's father-in-law, and then to the high priest.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEEvans2003487–500-285][^cite_note-FOOTNOTEBlomberg2009396–400-286][^cite_note-Holman608-287]

A depiction of Jesus's public trial

*Ecce homo!* [Antonio Ciseri](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Antonio_Ciseri)'s 1871 depiction of [Pontius Pilate](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pontius_Pilate) presenting Jesus to the public

During the trials Jesus speaks very little, mounts no defence, and gives very infrequent and indirect answers to the priests' questions, prompting an officer to slap him. In Matthew 26\:62, Jesus's unresponsiveness leads Caiaphas to ask him, "Have you no answer?".[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEEvans2003487–500-285][^cite_note-FOOTNOTEBlomberg2009396–400-286][^cite_note-Holman608-287] In Mark 14\:61, the high priest then asks Jesus, "Are you the Messiah, the Son of the Blessed One?". Jesus replies, "[I am](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/I_am_\(biblical_term\))", and then predicts the coming of the Son of Man.[^cite_note-Britannica-40] This provokes Caiaphas to tear his own robe in anger and to accuse Jesus of blasphemy. In Matthew and Luke, Jesus's answer is more ambiguous:[^cite_note-Britannica-40][^cite_note-FOOTNOTEEvans2003495-288] in Matthew 26\:64, he responds, "You have said so", and in Luke 22\:70 he says, "You say that I am."[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEBlomberg2009396–398-289][^cite_note-290]

The Jewish elders take Jesus to [Pilate's Court](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pilate's_Court) and ask the Roman governor, [Pontius Pilate](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pontius_Pilate), to judge and condemn Jesus for various allegations: subverting the nation, opposing the payment of tribute, claiming to be Christ, a king, and claiming to be the son of God.[^cite_note-291][^cite_note-Holman608-287] The use of the word "king" is central to the discussion between Jesus and Pilate. In John 18\:36, Jesus states, "My kingdom is not from this world", but he does not unequivocally deny being the King of the Jews.[^cite_note-292][^cite_note-293] In Luke 23\:7–15, Pilate realizes that Jesus is a Galilean, and thus comes under the jurisdiction of [Herod Antipas](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Herod_Antipas), the [Tetrarch](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Herodian_Tetrarchy) of Galilee and Perea.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTENiswonger1992172-294][^cite_note-FOOTNOTEMajerníkPonessaManhardt2005181-295] Pilate sends Jesus to Herod to be tried,[^cite_note-FOOTNOTECarter2003120–121-296] but Jesus says almost nothing in response to Herod's questions. Herod and his soldiers mock Jesus, put an expensive robe on him to make him look like a king, and return him to Pilate,[^cite_note-FOOTNOTENiswonger1992172-294] who then calls together the Jewish elders and announces that he has "not found this man guilty".[^cite_note-FOOTNOTECarter2003120–121-296]

Observing a Passover custom of the time, Pilate allows one prisoner chosen by the crowd to be released. He gives the people a choice between Jesus and a murderer called [Barabbas](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Barabbas) ([בר-אבא](https://en.wiktionary.org/wiki/%D7%91%D7%A8-%D7%90%D7%91%D7%90) or *Bar-abbâ*, "son of the father", from the common given name *[Abba](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Abba_\(given_name\))*: 'father').[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEEvans2012b453-297] Persuaded by the elders,[^cite_note-298] the mob chooses to release Barabbas and crucify Jesus.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEBlomberg2009400–401-299] Pilate writes a sign in Hebrew, Latin, and Greek that reads "Jesus of Nazareth, the King of the Jews" (abbreviated as [INRI](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/INRI) in depictions) to be affixed to Jesus's cross,[^cite_note-300][^cite_note-FOOTNOTEBrown198893-301] then [scourges Jesus](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Flagellation_of_Christ) and sends him to be crucified. The soldiers place a [crown of thorns](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Crown_of_thorns) on Jesus's head and ridicule him as the King of the Jews. They beat and taunt him before taking him to [Calvary](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Calvary),[^cite_note-302] also called Golgotha, for crucifixion.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEEvans2003487–500-285][^cite_note-Holman608-287][^cite_note-FOOTNOTEBlomberg2009402-303]

#### Crucifixion and entombment

Main articles: [Crucifixion of Jesus](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Crucifixion_of_Jesus) and [Burial of Jesus](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Burial_of_Jesus)

See also: [Sayings of Jesus on the cross](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sayings_of_Jesus_on_the_cross) and [Crucifixion darkness](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Crucifixion_darkness)

A depiction of Jesus on the cross.

[Diego Velázquez](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Diego_Vel%C3%A1zquez)'s depiction of the [Crucifixion](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Crucifixion_of_Jesus), approx. 1632

Jesus's crucifixion is described in all four canonical gospels. After the trials, Jesus is led to Calvary [carrying his cross](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Christ_Carrying_the_Cross); the route traditionally thought to have been taken is known as the [Via Dolorosa](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Via_Dolorosa). The three Synoptic Gospels indicate that [Simon of Cyrene](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Simon_of_Cyrene) assists him, having been compelled by the Romans to do so.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEEvans2003509–520-304][^cite_note-FOOTNOTEKöstenbergerKellumQuarles2009211–214-305] In Luke 23\:27–28, Jesus tells the women in the multitude of people following him not to weep for him but for themselves and their children.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEEvans2003509–520-304] At Calvary, Jesus is offered a sponge soaked in a concoction usually offered as a [painkiller](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Painkiller). According to Matthew and Mark, he refuses it.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEEvans2003509–520-304][^cite_note-FOOTNOTEKöstenbergerKellumQuarles2009211–214-305]

The soldiers then crucify Jesus and [cast lots](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sortes_\(ancient_Rome\)) for his clothes. Above Jesus's head on the cross is Pilate's multilingual inscription, "Jesus of Nazareth, the King of the Jews." Soldiers and passersby [mock](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mocking_of_Jesus) him about it. Two convicted thieves are crucified along with Jesus. In Matthew and Mark, both thieves mock Jesus. In Luke, [one of them](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Impenitent_thief) rebukes Jesus, while [the other](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Penitent_thief) defends him.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEEvans2003509–520-304][^cite_note-FOOTNOTEDoninger1999271-306][^cite_note-FOOTNOTEEhrman2009\[httpsarchiveorgdetailsjesusinterrupted00ehrm_0page82_82\]-307] Jesus tells the latter: "today you will be with me in Paradise."[^cite_note-308] The four gospels mention the presence of [a group of female disciples of Jesus](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Women_at_the_crucifixion) at the crucifixion. In John, Jesus sees his mother Mary and the [beloved disciple](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Beloved_disciple) and tells him to take care of her.[^cite_note-309]

In John 19\:33–34, Roman soldiers break the two thieves' legs to hasten their death, but not those of Jesus, as he is already dead. Instead, [one soldier](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Longinus) pierces Jesus's side with a [lance](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Holy_Lance), and [blood and water](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Five_Holy_Wounds) flow out.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEDoninger1999271-306] The Synoptics report a [period of darkness](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Crucifixion_darkness), and the heavy curtain in the Temple is torn when Jesus dies. In Matthew 27\:51–54, an earthquake breaks open tombs. In Matthew and Mark, terrified by the events, a Roman [centurion](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Centurion) states that Jesus was the Son of God.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEEvans2003509–520-304][^cite_note-FOOTNOTEKöstenbergerKellumQuarles2009213–214-310]

On the same day, [Joseph of Arimathea](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Joseph_of_Arimathea), with Pilate's permission and with [Nicodemus](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nicodemus)'s help, [removes Jesus's body from the cross](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Descent_from_the_Cross), wraps it in a clean cloth, and buries it in a new [rock-hewn tomb](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sepulchre).[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEEvans2003509–520-304] In Matthew 27\:62–66, on the following day the chief Jewish priests ask Pilate for the tomb to be secured, and with Pilate's permission the priests place seals on the large stone covering the entrance.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEEvans2003509–520-304][^cite_note-FOOTNOTEMorris1992727-311]

### Resurrection and ascension

Main articles: [Resurrection of Jesus](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Resurrection_of_Jesus), [Empty tomb](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Empty_tomb), and [Ascension of Jesus](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ascension_of_Jesus)

Further information: [Overview of resurrection appearances in the Gospels and Paul](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Overview_of_resurrection_appearances_in_the_Gospels_and_Paul)

See also: [Resurrection of Jesus in Christian art](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Resurrection_of_Jesus_in_Christian_art) and [Ascension of Jesus in Christian art](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ascension_of_Jesus_in_Christian_art)

Jesus appearing to Mary Magdalene after his resurrection from the dead, depicted by Alexander Andreyevich Ivanov.

*Appearance of Jesus Christ to Maria Magdalena* by [Alexander Andreyevich Ivanov](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Alexander_Andreyevich_Ivanov), 1835

The Gospels do not describe the moment of the [resurrection of Jesus](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Resurrection_of_Jesus). They describe the discovery of his [empty tomb](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Empty_tomb) and several appearances of Jesus, with distinct differences in each narrative.[^cite_note-312]

In the four Gospels, [Mary Magdalene](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mary_Magdalene) goes to the tomb on Sunday morning, alone or with one or several [other women](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Myrrhbearers).[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEHarris1985308–309-313] The tomb is empty, with the stone rolled away, and there are one or two angels, depending on the accounts. In the Synoptics, the women are told that Jesus is not here and that he is risen.[^cite_note-314] In Mark and Matthew, the angel also instructs them to tell the disciples to meet Jesus in Galilee.[^cite_note-315] In Luke, Peter visits the tomb after he is told it is empty.[^cite_note-316] In John, he goes there with the beloved disciple.[^cite_note-317] Matthew mentions Roman guards at the tomb,[^cite_note-318] who report to the priests of Jerusalem what happened. The priests bribe them to say that the disciples [stole Jesus's body](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Stolen_body_hypothesis) during the night.[^cite_note-319]

The four Gospels then describe various appearances of Jesus in his resurrected body. Jesus first reveals himself to Mary Magdalene in Mark 16\:9 and John 20\:14–17,[^cite_note-320] along with "the other Mary" in Matthew 28\:9,[^cite_note-321] while in Luke the first reported appearance is to [two disciples](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Road_to_Emmaus_appearance) heading to [Emmaus](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Emmaus).[^cite_note-322] Jesus then reveals himself to the eleven disciples, in Jerusalem or in Galilee.[^cite_note-323] In Luke 24\:36–43, he eats and shows them his [tangible wounds](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Five_Holy_Wounds) to prove that he is not a spirit.[^cite_note-324] He also shows them to [Thomas](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Thomas_the_Apostle) to end [his doubts](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Doubting_Thomas), in John 20\:24–29.[^cite_note-325] In the Synoptics, Jesus [commissions the disciples](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Great_Commission) to spread the gospel message to all nations,[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEHarris1985272–285-138][^cite_note-FOOTNOTEHarris1985297–301-326] while in [John 21](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/John_21), he [tells Peter](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Restoration_of_Peter) to take care of his sheep.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEEvans2003521–530-74][^cite_note-FOOTNOTECoxEasley2007216–226-327]

Jesus's [ascension into Heaven](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Entering_Heaven_alive) is described in Luke 24\:50–53, Acts 1\:1–11, and mentioned in 1 Timothy 3\:16. In the Acts of the Apostles, forty days after the Resurrection, as the disciples look on, "he was lifted up, and a cloud took him out of their sight". 1 Peter 3\:22 states that Jesus has "gone into heaven and is at the right hand of God".[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEEvans2003521–530-74]

The Acts of the Apostles describes several appearances of Jesus after his Ascension. In Acts 7\:55, [Stephen](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Saint_Stephen) gazes into heaven and sees "Jesus standing at the right hand of God" just before his death.[^cite_note-328] On the road to [Damascus](8914.md), the Apostle [Paul is converted](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Conversion_of_Paul_the_Apostle) to Christianity after seeing a blinding light and hearing a voice saying, "I am Jesus, whom you are persecuting."[^cite_note-329] In Acts 9\:10–18, Jesus instructs [Ananias of Damascus](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ananias_of_Damascus) in a vision to heal Paul.[^cite_note-330] The [Book of Revelation](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Book_of_Revelation) includes a revelation from Jesus concerning the [last days of Earth](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Christian_eschatology).[^cite_note-331]

## Early Christianity

Main article: [Early Christianity](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Early_Christianity)



3rd-century depiction of Jesus as the [Good Shepherd](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Good_Shepherd)

After Jesus's life, his followers, as described in the first chapters of the [Acts of the Apostles](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Acts_of_the_Apostles), were all Jews either by birth or [conversion](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Conversion_to_Judaism), for which the biblical term "[proselyte](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Proselyte)" is used,[^cite_note-332] and referred to by historians as [Jewish Christians](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jewish_Christian). The early Gospel message was spread [orally](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Oral_gospel_traditions), probably in Aramaic,[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEEhrman201287–90-333] but almost immediately also in [Greek](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Koine_Greek).[^cite_note-334] The [New Testament](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/New_Testament)'s Acts of the Apostles and [Epistle to the Galatians](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Epistle_to_the_Galatians) record that the first Christian community was [centred in Jerusalem](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/First_Christian_church) and its leaders included [Peter](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Saint_Peter), [James, the brother of Jesus](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/James_\(brother_of_Jesus\)), and [John the Apostle](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/John_the_Apostle).[^cite_note-335]

After [his conversion](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Conversion_of_Paul_the_Apostle), [Paul the Apostle](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Paul_the_Apostle) spread the teachings of Jesus to various [non-Jewish](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Non-Jewish) communities throughout the eastern Mediterranean region. Paul's influence on Christian thinking is said to be more significant than that of any other [New Testament author](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Authorship_of_the_New_Testament).[^cite_note-336] By the end of the [1st century](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Christianity_in_the_1st_century), Christianity began to be recognized internally and externally as a [separate religion from Judaism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Split_of_early_Christianity_and_Judaism) which itself was refined and developed further in the centuries after the [destruction of the Second Temple](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Siege_of_Jerusalem_\(70_AD\)).[^cite_note-337]

Numerous quotations in the New Testament and other Christian writings of the first centuries indicate that early Christians generally used and revered the [Hebrew Bible](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hebrew_Bible) (the [Tanakh](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tanakh)) as [religious text](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Religious_text), mostly in the Greek ([Septuagint](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Septuagint)) or Aramaic ([Targum](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Targum)) translations.[^cite_note-338] Early Christians wrote many religious works, including the ones included in the [canon of the New Testament](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Canon_of_the_New_Testament). The canonical texts, which have become the main sources used by historians to try to understand the historical Jesus and sacred texts within Christianity, were probably written between AD 50 and 120.[^cite_note-339]

## Historical views

Main articles: [Historical Jesus](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Historical_Jesus), [Quest for the historical Jesus](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Quest_for_the_historical_Jesus), and [Scholarly interpretation of Gospel elements](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Scholarly_interpretation_of_Gospel_elements)

See also: [Biblical criticism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Biblical_criticism)

Prior to [the Enlightenment](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Age_of_Enlightenment), the Gospels were usually regarded as accurate historical accounts, but since then scholars have emerged who question the reliability of the Gospels and draw a distinction between the Jesus described in the Gospels and the Jesus of history.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTELevine20065-340] Since the 18th century, three separate scholarly quests for the historical Jesus have taken place, each with distinct characteristics and based on different research criteria, which were often developed during the quest that applied them.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEWitherington1997113-107][^cite_note-FOOTNOTEPowell199819–23-341] While there is widespread scholarly agreement on the existence of Jesus,[^cite_note-exist-32] and a basic consensus on the general outline of his life,[^cite_note-343] the portraits of Jesus constructed by various scholars often differ from each other, and from the image portrayed in the gospel accounts.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTETheissenWinter20025-344][^cite_note-345]

Approaches to the historical reconstruction of the life of Jesus have varied from the "maximalist" approaches of the 19th century, in which the gospel accounts were accepted as reliable evidence wherever it is possible, to the "minimalist" approaches of the early 20th century, where hardly anything about Jesus was accepted as historical.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEKeener2012163-346] In the 1950s, as the second quest for the historical Jesus gathered pace, the minimalist approaches faded away, and in the 21st century, minimalists such as [Price](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Robert_M._Price) are a small minority.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEChiltonEvans199827-347][^cite_note-FOOTNOTEEvans2012a4–5-348] Although a belief in the [inerrancy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Biblical_inerrancy) of the Gospels cannot be supported historically, many scholars since the 1980s have held that, beyond the few facts considered to be historically certain, certain other elements of Jesus's life are "historically probable".[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEChiltonEvans199827-347][^cite_note-349][^cite_note-FOOTNOTETheissenWinter2002142–143-350] Modern scholarly research on the historical Jesus thus focuses on identifying the most probable elements.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEAndersonJustThatcher2007131-351][^cite_note-FOOTNOTEMeier2006124-352]

### Judea and Galilee in the 1st century

Further information: [Historical background of the New Testament](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Historical_background_of_the_New_Testament)

Topographical map of Palestine in the 1st century highlighting places mentioned in the canonical gospels.

[Judea](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Judea), [Galilee](12639.md) and neighbouring areas at the time of Jesus

In AD 6, Judea, [Idumea](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Idumea), and [Samaria](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Samaria) were transformed from a [client state](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Client_kingdoms_in_ancient_Rome) of the [Roman Empire](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Roman_Empire) into an imperial province, also called [Judea](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Judea_\(Roman_province\)). A Roman [prefect](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Prefect), rather than a client ruler, governed the land. The prefect governed from [Caesarea Maritima](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Caesarea_Maritima), leaving Jerusalem to be run by the [High Priest of Israel](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_High_Priests_of_Israel).[^cite_note-horsley-353] As an exception, the prefect came to Jerusalem during religious festivals, when religious and patriotic enthusiasm sometimes inspired unrest or uprisings. [Galilee](12639.md) with [Perea](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Perea) was a Herodian client state under the rule of [Herod Antipas](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Herod_Antipas) since 4 BC.[^cite_note-horsley-353] Galilee was evidently prosperous, and poverty was limited enough that it did not threaten the social order.[^cite_note-Britannica-40] [Philip](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Philip_the_Tetrarch) (d. AD 34), half-brother of Herod Antipas, ruled as [Tetrarch](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Herodian_tetrarchy) yet another Herodian client state to the north and east of the sea of Galilee that included [Gaulanitis](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gaulanitis), [Batanea](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Batanea), and [Iturea](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Iturea); it was mostly non-Jewish.[^cite_note-354] South of this on the east bank of the Jordan was the [Decapolis](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Decapolis); a collection of Hellenistic city-states that were clients of the Roman Empire. North of Galilee were the cities of [Tyre](30796.md) and [Sidon](28366.md) which were in the Roman province of [Syria](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Roman_Syria). Though non-Jewish lands surrounded the mostly Jewish territories of Judea and Galilee, Roman law and practice allowed Jews to remain separate legally and culturally.[^cite_note-Britannica-40]

This was the era of [Hellenistic Judaism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hellenistic_Judaism), which combined [Jewish religious tradition](15624.md) with elements of [Hellenistic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hellenistic) culture. Until the [fall of the Western Roman Empire](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fall_of_the_Western_Roman_Empire) and the [Muslim conquests](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Early_Muslim_conquests) of the Eastern Mediterranean, the main centres of Hellenistic Judaism were [Alexandria](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Alexandria) (Egypt) and [Antioch](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Antioch) (now Southern Turkey), the two main [Greek colonies](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Greek_colonies) of the [Middle East and North Africa](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Middle_East_and_North_Africa) area, both founded at the end of the 4th century BC in the wake of the conquests of [Alexander the Great](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Alexander_the_Great). Hellenistic Judaism also existed in [Jerusalem during the Second Temple Period](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jerusalem_during_the_Second_Temple_Period), where there was conflict between Hellenizers and traditionalists (sometimes called [Judaizers](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Judaizers)). The [Hebrew Bible](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hebrew_Bible) was translated from [Biblical Hebrew](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Biblical_Hebrew) and [Biblical Aramaic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Biblical_Aramaic) into [Jewish Koine Greek](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jewish_Koine_Greek); the [Targum](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Targum) translations into Aramaic were also generated during this era, both due to the decline of knowledge of Hebrew.[^cite_note-355]

Jews based their faith and religious practice on the [Torah](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Torah), five books said to have been given by God to [Moses](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Moses). The three prominent religious parties were the [Pharisees](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pharisees), the [Essenes](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Essenes), and the [Sadducees](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sadducees). Together these parties represented only a small fraction of the population. Most Jews looked forward to a time when God would deliver them from their pagan rulers, possibly through war against the Romans.[^cite_note-Britannica-40]

### Sources

Main article: [Sources for the historicity of Jesus](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sources_for_the_historicity_of_Jesus)

See also: [Josephus on Jesus](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Josephus_on_Jesus) and [Tacitus on Jesus](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tacitus_on_Jesus)



A 1582 edition of the works of [Josephus](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Josephus), a 1st-century [Roman-Jewish](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jews_in_the_Roman_Empire) historian who [referred to Jesus](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Josephus_on_Jesus)

[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEBlomberg2009431–436-356]

New Testament scholars face a formidable challenge when they analyse the canonical Gospels.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEHarris1985263-357] The Gospels are not biographies in the modern sense, and the authors explain Jesus's theological significance and recount his public ministry while omitting many details of his life.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEHarris1985263-357] [James Dunn](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/James_Dunn_\(theologian\)) has argued that the accounts of his teachings and life were [initially conserved by oral transmission](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Oral_gospel_traditions), which was the source of the written Gospels.[^cite_note-Dunn2013-188] The Gospels are commonly seen as literature that is based on oral traditions, Christian preaching, and Old Testament exegesis with the consensus being that they are a variation of Greco-Roman biography; similar to other ancient works such as Xenophon's [*Memoirs of Socrates*](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Memorabilia_\(Xenophon\)).[^cite_note-358]

The reports of supernatural events associated with Jesus's death and resurrection make the challenge even more difficult.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEHarris1985263-357] Scholars regard the Gospels as compromised sources of information because the writers were trying to glorify Jesus.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTESanders19933-99] Ed Sanders argues that surviving textual sources provide more reliable details for Jesus's thoughts than they do for the thoughts of [Alexander the Great](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Alexander_the_Great), owing to the texts discussing Jesus being authored closer in time to the events they relate to. Biographies written about Alexander the Great during his own lifetime (who lived 330 years earlier) have all been lost, but are known of through references in biographies written by later authors. Although the texts about Jesus contain ideas from both Jesus and his later followers, it is possible to distinguish which parts originate from Jesus's own view, and which were ideas from his later followers.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTESanders19933-99]

Scholars use several criteria, such as the [criterion of independent attestation](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Criterion_of_independent_attestation), the [criterion of coherence](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Biblical_criticism#Coherence), and the [criterion of discontinuity](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Criterion_of_discontinuity) to judge the historicity of events.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTERausch200336–37-359] The historicity of an event also depends on the reliability of the source; indeed, the Gospels are not independent nor consistent records of Jesus's life. The [Synoptics](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Synoptic_Gospels), especially Mark, the earliest written gospel, have been considered the most reliable sources of information about Jesus for many decades.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTESanders199371-360][^cite_note-FOOTNOTETheissenMerz199817–62-184][^cite_note-Britannica-40][^cite_note-361] John, the latest written gospel, differs considerably from the Synoptic Gospels, and has been considered less reliable, although John's gospel is seen as having more reliability than previously thought or sometimes even more reliable than the synoptics since the [third quest](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Quest_for_the_historical_Jesus#Third_quest).[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEAndersonJustThatcher2007292-362][^cite_note-rout-363][^cite_note-john_JH-364][^cite_note-365]

Some scholars (such as the [Jesus Seminar](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jesus_Seminar)) believe that the non-canonical [Gospel of Thomas](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gospel_of_Thomas) might be an independent witness to many of Jesus's parables and aphorisms. For example, Thomas confirms that Jesus blessed the poor and that this saying circulated independently before being combined with similar sayings in the [Q source](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Q_source).[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEFunkHooverThe_Jesus_Seminar1993471–532-366] The majority of scholars are sceptical about this text and believe it should be dated to the 2nd century AD.[^cite_note-367][^cite_note-368] Other select non-canonical Christian texts may also have value for historical Jesus research.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTETheissenMerz199817–62-184]

Early non-Christian sources that attest to the historical existence of Jesus include the works of the historians [Josephus](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Josephus) and [Tacitus](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tacitus).[^cite_note-370][^cite_note-FOOTNOTEBlomberg2009431–436-356][^cite_note-FOOTNOTEVan_Voorst200039–53-371] Josephus scholar [Louis Feldman](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Louis_Feldman) has stated that "few have doubted the genuineness" of Josephus's reference to Jesus in [book 20](https://en.wikisource.org/wiki/The%20Antiquities%20of%20the%20Jews/Book%20XX#Chapter_9) of the *[Antiquities of the Jews](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Antiquities_of_the_Jews)*, and it is disputed only by a small number of scholars.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEVan_Voorst200083-372][^cite_note-373] Tacitus referred to Christ and his execution by Pilate in [book 15](https://en.wikisource.org/wiki/The%20Annals%20\(Tacitus\)/Book%2015#44) of his work *[Annals](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Annals_\(Tacitus\))*. Scholars generally consider Tacitus's reference to the execution of Jesus to be both authentic and of historical value as an independent Roman source.[^cite_note-374]

Non-Christian sources are valuable as they show that even neutral or hostile parties never show any doubt that Jesus existed. They present a rough picture of Jesus that is compatible with that found in the Christian sources: that Jesus was a teacher, had a reputation as a miracle worker, had a brother James, and died a violent death.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTETheissenMerz1998-375]

Archaeology helps scholars better understand Jesus's social world.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEReed200218-376] For example, it indicates that [Capernaum](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Capernaum), a city important in Jesus's ministry, was poor and small, without even a [forum](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Forum_\(Roman\)) or an [agora](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Agora).[^cite_note-Gowler-377][^cite_note-378] This archaeological discovery resonates well with the scholarly view that Jesus advocated reciprocal sharing among the destitute in that area of Galilee.[^cite_note-Gowler-377]
