Jesus was a Galilean Jew,[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEVermes198120,_26,_27,_29-18] born around the beginning of the 1st century, who died in AD 30 or 33 in [Judea](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Judea_\(Roman_province\)).[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEHumphreysWaddington1992340-379] The general scholarly consensus is that Jesus was a contemporary of John the Baptist and was crucified as ordered by the Roman governor Pontius Pilate,[^cite_note-FOOTNOTELevine20064-222] who held office from AD 26 to 36.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTELevine20064-222]

The Gospels offer several indications concerning the year of Jesus's birth. Matthew 2\:1 associates the birth of Jesus with the reign of [Herod the Great](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Herod_the_Great), who died around 4 BC, and Luke 1\:5 mentions that Herod was on the throne shortly before the birth of Jesus,[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEMaier1989115–118-380][^cite_note-FOOTNOTENiswonger1992121–122-381] although this gospel also associates the birth with the [Census of Quirinius](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Census_of_Quirinius) which took place ten years later.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEKöstenbergerKellumQuarles2009137–138-382][^cite_note-FOOTNOTENiswonger1992122–124-383] Luke 3\:23 states that Jesus was "about thirty years old" at the start of his [ministry](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ministry_of_Jesus), which according to Acts 10\:37–38 was preceded by John the Baptist's ministry, which was recorded in Luke 3\:1–2 to have begun in the 15th year of [Tiberius](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tiberius)'s reign (AD 28 or 29).[^cite_note-FOOTNOTENiswonger1992121–122-381][^cite_note-Vermes-2010-384] By collating the gospel accounts with historical data and using various other methods, most scholars arrive at a [date of birth for Jesus](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Date_of_the_birth_of_Jesus) between 6 and 4 BC,[^cite_note-Vermes-2010-384][^cite_note-FOOTNOTEDunn2003324-385] but some propose estimates that include a wider range.[^cite_note-386]

The date range for Jesus's ministry has been estimated using several different approaches.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEKöstenbergerKellumQuarles2009140-387][^cite_note-FOOTNOTEFreedman2000249-388] One of these applies the reference in Luke 3\:1–2, Acts 10\:37–38, and the dates of Tiberius's reign, which are well known, to give a date of around AD 28–29 for the start of Jesus's ministry.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEMaier1989120–121-389] Another approach estimates a date around AD 27–29 by using the statement about the temple in John 2\:13–20, which asserts that the temple in Jerusalem was in its 46th year of construction at the start of Jesus's ministry, together with Josephus's statement[^cite_note-390] that the temple's reconstruction was started by Herod the Great in the 18th year of his reign.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEKöstenbergerKellumQuarles2009140-387][^cite_note-FOOTNOTEMaier1989123-391] A further method uses the date of the [death of John the Baptist](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Death_of_John_the_Baptist) and the marriage of [Herod Antipas](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Herod_Antipas) to [Herodias](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Herodias), based on the writings of Josephus, and correlates it with Matthew 14\:4 and Mark 6\:18.[^cite_note-392][^cite_note-393] Given that most scholars date the marriage of Herod and Herodias as AD 28–35, this yields a date about AD 28–29.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEFreedman2000249-388]

Various approaches have been used to estimate the year of the crucifixion of Jesus. Most scholars agree that he died in AD 30 or 33.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEHumphreysWaddington1992340-379][^cite_note-FOOTNOTEKöstenbergerKellumQuarles2009398-394] The Gospels state that the event occurred during the prefecture of Pilate.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTETheissenMerz199881–83-395][^cite_note-396][^cite_note-FOOTNOTECarter200344–45-397] The date for the [conversion of Paul](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Conversion_of_Paul) (estimated to be AD 33–36) acts as an upper bound for the date of Crucifixion. The dates for Paul's conversion and ministry can be determined by analysing the [Pauline epistles](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pauline_epistles) and the Acts of the Apostles.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEKöstenbergerKellumQuarles2009398–400-398][^cite_note-399] Astronomers have tried to estimate the precise date of the Crucifixion by analysing lunar motion and calculating historic dates of [Passover](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Passover), a festival based on the lunisolar [Hebrew calendar](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hebrew_calendar). The most widely accepted dates derived from this method are 7 April AD 30, and 3 April AD 33 (both [Julian](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Julian_calendar)).[^cite_note-400]

### Historicity of events

Main article: [Historicity of Jesus](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Historicity_of_Jesus)

See also: [History of the Jews in the Roman Empire](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/History_of_the_Jews_in_the_Roman_Empire), [Historical criticism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Historical_criticism), [Textual criticism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Textual_criticism), and [Historical reliability of the Gospels](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Historical_reliability_of_the_Gospels)

A white statue of a man

An apparently ancient document

Roman senator and historian [Tacitus](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tacitus) (statue, pictured left) mentioned the execution of "Christus" ('Jesus') by Pilate in a passage describing the [Great Fire of Rome](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Great_Fire_of_Rome) and [Nero](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nero)'s persecution of Christians in the *[Annals](https://en.wikisource.org/wiki/The%20Annals%20\(Tacitus\)/Book%2015#44)*, a history of the Roman Empire during the 1st century.

Nearly all historians (both modern and historical) agree that Jesus was a real person who historically existed.[^cite_note-exist-32] Scholars have reached a limited consensus on the basics of Jesus's life.[^cite_note-White-401]

#### Family

See also: [Brothers of Jesus](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Brothers_of_Jesus)

Many scholars including biblical ones agree that Joseph, Jesus's father, died before Jesus began his ministry. Joseph is not mentioned in the Gospels during Jesus's ministry. Joseph's death would explain why in Mark 6\:3, Jesus's neighbours refer to Jesus as the "son of Mary" (sons were usually identified by their fathers).[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEBrown1978\[httpsbooksgooglecombooksidML1mnUBwmhcCpgPA64_64\]-402]

According to Theissen and Merz, it is common for extraordinary [charismatic leaders](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Charismatic_leaders), such as Jesus, to come into conflict with their ordinary families.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTETheissenMerz1998194-403] In Mark, Jesus's family comes to get him, fearing that he is mad (Mark 3\:20–34), and this account is thought to be historical because early Christians would probably not have invented it.[^cite_note-404] After Jesus's death, many members of his family joined the Christian movement.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTETheissenMerz1998194-403] Jesus's brother [James](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/James,_brother_of_Jesus) became a leader of the Jerusalem Church.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTECrossLivingstone2005James,_St.-405]

[Géza Vermes](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/G%C3%A9za_Vermes) says that the doctrine of the [virgin birth of Jesus](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Virgin_birth_of_Jesus) arose from theological development rather than from historical events.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEVermes1981283-406] Other scholars take it as significant that the virgin birth is [attested](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Criterion_of_multiple_attestation) by two separate gospels, Matthew and Luke.[^cite_note-407][^cite_note-FOOTNOTEKeener2009b\[httpsbooksgooglecombooksid8C2Y_HaL5W0CpgPA83_83\]-408][^cite_note-409][^cite_note-410][^cite_note-411][^cite_note-412]

[E. P. Sanders](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/E._P._Sanders) and Marcus Borg note that the [birth narratives](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nativity_of_Jesus) in [Matthew](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gospel_of_Matthew) and [Luke](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gospel_of_Luke) are ahistorical and the clearest cases of invention in the Gospel narratives of Jesus's life.[^cite_note-413] [Dale Allison](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dale_Allison) and [W. D. Davies](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/W._D._Davies) argue that Matthew presents a unified and preexisting infancy narrative based on haggadic legends about [Moses](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Moses), though they maintain that elements in the story such as the names of Mary and Joseph and Jesus being in Nazareth during [Herod](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Herod_the_Great)'s reign are historical.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTECrossanWatts1999\[httpsarchiveorgdetailswhoisjesusanswer00crospage11_11–12\]-414] Both accounts have Jesus born in [Bethlehem](4312.md), in accordance with Jewish salvation history, and both have him growing up in Nazareth, but Sanders points out that the two report different explanations for how that happened. Luke's account of a worldwide census is not plausible, while Matthew's account is more plausible, but the story reads as though it was invented to identify Jesus as a new Moses, and the historian [Josephus](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Josephus) reports Herod the Great's brutality without ever mentioning that [he massacred little boys](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Massacre_of_the_Innocents).[^cite_note-FOOTNOTESanders199385–88-415] Scholars including [Gerd Theissen](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gerd_Theissen) and [Annette Merz](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Annette_Merz) conclude that Jesus was born in [Nazareth](21702.md), and the Bethlehem birth narrative was developed to align Jesus's story with scripture foretelling that the messiah would be born in Bethlehem.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTETheissenMerz1998191-416][^cite_note-417] The differences found in the gospel accounts are typical of ancient historical biographies.[^cite_note-418] The contradictions were apparent to early Christians, with harmonizations present in the [infancy gospels](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Infancy_gospels) of [Thomas](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Infancy_Gospel_of_Thomas) and the [Gospel of James](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gospel_of_James), which are dated to the 2nd century AD.[^cite_note-419][^cite_note-420]

Conservative scholars argue that despite the uncertainty of the details, the gospel birth narratives trace back to historical, or at least much earlier pre-gospel traditions.[^cite_note-421][^cite_note-422][^cite_note-FOOTNOTEBlomberg2009243–244-423][^cite_note-FOOTNOTEBrown1977104–121-424] For instance, according to [Ben Witherington](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ben_Witherington):

> What we find in Matthew and Luke is not the story of ... a \[god] descending to earth and, in the guise of a man, mating with a human woman, but rather the story of a miraculous conception without the aid of any man, divine or otherwise. As such, this story is without precedent either in Jewish or pagan literature.[^cite_note-425]

Sanders says that the genealogies of Jesus are based not on historical information but on the author's desire to show that Jesus was the universal Jewish saviour.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTESanders199380–91-121] In any event, once the doctrine of the virgin birth of Jesus became established, that tradition superseded the earlier tradition that he was descended from [David](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/David) through Joseph.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTETheissenMerz1998196-426] The Gospel of Luke reports that Jesus was a [blood relative](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Blood_relative) of John the Baptist, but scholars generally consider this connection to be invented.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTESanders199380–91-121][^cite_note-427]

#### Baptism



Baptism in the [Jordan River](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jordan_River), the river where Jesus was baptized

Most modern scholars consider Jesus's baptism to be a historical fact, along with his crucifixion.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEDunn2003339-10] The theologian [James D. G. Dunn](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/James_D._G._Dunn) states that they "command almost universal assent" and "rank so high on the 'almost impossible to doubt or deny' scale of historical facts" that they are often the starting points for the study of the historical Jesus.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEDunn2003339-10] Scholars adduce the [criterion of embarrassment](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Criterion_of_embarrassment), saying that early Christians would not have invented a baptism that might imply that Jesus committed [sins](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sin) and wanted to [repent](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Repent).[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEPowell199847-428][^cite_note-429] According to Theissen and Merz, Jesus was inspired by John the Baptist and took over from him many elements of his teaching.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTETheissenMerz1998235-430]

#### Ministry in Galilee

Most scholars hold that Jesus lived in Galilee and Judea and did not preach or study elsewhere.[^cite_note-431] They agree that Jesus debated with Jewish authorities on the subject of God, performed some healings, taught in [parables](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Parable) and gathered followers.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTELevine20064-222] Jesus's Jewish critics considered his ministry to be scandalous because he feasted with sinners, fraternized with women, and allowed his followers to pluck grain on the Sabbath.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEFunkHooverThe_Jesus_Seminar19933-82] According to Sanders, it is not plausible that disagreements over how to interpret the Law of Moses and the Sabbath would have led Jewish authorities to want Jesus killed.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTESanders1993205–223-432]

According to Ehrman, Jesus taught that a coming kingdom was everyone's proper focus, not anything in this life.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEEhrman1999167–170-433] He taught about the Jewish Law, seeking its true meaning, sometimes in opposition to traditions. Jesus put love at the centre of the Law, and following that Law was an apocalyptic necessity.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEEhrman1999164–167-434] His ethical teachings called for forgiveness, not judging others, loving enemies, and caring for the poor.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEEhrman1999171–176-435] Funk and Hoover note that typical of Jesus were [paradoxical](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Paradox) or surprising turns of phrase, such as advising one, when struck on the cheek, [to offer the other cheek](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Turning_the_other_cheek) to be struck as well.[^cite_note-436][^cite_note-FOOTNOTEFunkHooverThe_Jesus_Seminar1993294-437]

The Gospels portray Jesus teaching in well-defined sessions, such as the Sermon on the Mount in the Gospel of Matthew or the parallel [Sermon on the Plain](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sermon_on_the_Plain) in Luke. While these teaching sessions include authentic teachings of Jesus, Theissen and Merz contend that the scenes were invented by the evangelists to frame these teachings, originally recorded without context.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTETheissenMerz199817–62-184] Le Donne, however, rejects the [form-critical](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Form_criticism) notion that smaller units of traditions held a defined stage of circulation before the gospels' composition.[^cite_note-438] While Jesus's miracles fit within the social context of [antiquity](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ancient_history), he defined them differently. First, he attributed them to the faith of those healed. Second, he connected them to [end times](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/End_times) prophecy.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTETheissenMerz1998310-439]

Jesus chose [twelve disciples](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Twelve_disciples) (the "Twelve").[^cite_note-FOOTNOTESanders199310-440] According to Bart Ehrman, Jesus's promise that the Twelve would rule is historical, because the Twelve included [Judas Iscariot](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Judas_Iscariot). In Ehrman's view, no Christians would have invented a line from Jesus, promising rulership to the disciple who betrayed him.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEEhrman1999186–187-441]

In Mark, the disciples play hardly any role other than a negative one. While others sometimes respond to Jesus with complete faith, his disciples are puzzled and doubtful. They serve as a [foil](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Foil_\(literature\)) to Jesus and to other characters. The failings of the disciples are probably exaggerated in Mark, and the disciples make a better showing in Matthew and Luke.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTESanders1993123–124-442] Recent studies tend to suggest that Mark is not as negative towards [Peter](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Saint_Peter) as a previous generation of scholars thought.[^cite_note-443]

Sanders says that Jesus's mission was not about [repentance](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Repentance), although he acknowledges that this opinion is unpopular. He argues that repentance appears as a strong theme only in Luke, that repentance was John the Baptist's message, and that Jesus's ministry would not have been scandalous if the sinners he ate with had been repentant.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTESanders1993230–236-444] According to Theissen and Merz, Jesus taught that God was generously giving people an opportunity to repent.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTETheissenMerz1998336-445]

#### Role

Jesus referred to himself as a "[son of man](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Son_of_man)" in the colloquial sense of "a person", but scholars do not know whether he also meant himself when he referred to the heavenly "Son of Man". Paul the Apostle and other early Christians interpreted the "Son of Man" as the risen Jesus.[^cite_note-Britannica-40] [Dale Allison](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dale_Allison) argues Jesus identified himself as the son of man in Daniel, rejecting the notion of another eschatological figure.[^cite_note-446]

The Gospels refer to Jesus not only as a messiah but in the absolute form as "the Messiah" or, equivalently, "the Christ". In early Judaism, this absolute form of the title is not found, but only phrases such as "his messiah". The tradition is ambiguous enough to leave room for debate as to whether Jesus defined his [eschatological](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Eschatological) role as that of the Messiah.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTECrossLivingstone2005Messiah-447] The Jewish messianic tradition included many different forms, some of them focused on a messiah figure and others not. Based on the Christian tradition, [Gerd Theissen](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gerd_Theissen) advances the hypothesis that Jesus saw himself in messianic terms but did not claim the title "Messiah".[^cite_note-FOOTNOTETheissenMerz1998533–540-448] Bart Ehrman argues that Jesus did consider himself to be the Messiah, albeit in the sense that he would be the king of the new political order that God would usher in,[^cite_note-449] not in the sense that most people today think of the term.[^cite_note-450]

#### Passover and crucifixion in Jerusalem

Around AD 30, Jesus and his followers travelled from Galilee to Jerusalem to observe Passover.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTESanders199310-440] Jesus caused a disturbance in the [Second Temple](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Second_Temple),[^cite_note-FOOTNOTESanders199311-33] which was the centre of Jewish religious and civil authority. Sanders associates it with Jesus's prophecy that the Temple would be totally demolished.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTESanders1993254–262-451] Jesus held a last meal with his disciples, which is the origin of the [Sacrament of the Holy Eucharist](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Eucharist). According to [John P. Meier](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/John_P._Meier), Jesus having a final meal with his disciples is generally accepted among scholars, and belongs to the framework of the narrative of Jesus' life, with a majority viewing Mark 14 as substantially historical.[^cite_note-452] The meal appears to have pointed to Jesus's place in the Kingdom of God when Jesus probably knew he was to be killed, although he may have still hoped that God might intervene.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTESanders1993263–264-453]

The Gospels say that Jesus was betrayed to the authorities by a disciple, and many scholars consider this report to be highly reliable.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTECrossLivingstone2005Jesus_Christ-181] He was executed on the orders of Pontius Pilate, the Roman prefect of Judaea.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTESanders199311-33] Pilate most likely saw Jesus's reference to the Kingdom of God as a threat to Roman authority and worked with the Temple elites to have Jesus executed.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTETheissenMerz1998465–466-454] The Sadducean high-priestly leaders of the Temple more plausibly had Jesus executed for political reasons than for his teaching.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTECrossLivingstone2005Jesus_Christ-181] They may have regarded him as a threat to stability, especially after he caused a disturbance at the Second Temple.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTECrossLivingstone2005Jesus_Christ-181][^cite_note-JE1906-455] Other factors, such as Jesus's triumphal entry into Jerusalem, may have contributed to this decision.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTESanders1993269–273-456] Most scholars consider Jesus's crucifixion to be factual because early Christians would not have invented the painful death of their leader.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEDunn2003339-10][^cite_note-FOOTNOTEMeier2006126–128-457]

#### After crucifixion



The Resurrection of Christ from a 16th-century manuscript of *La Passion de Nostre Seigneur*

After Jesus's death, his followers said he was restored to life, although the exact details of their experiences are unclear. The gospel reports contradict each other; Sanders suggests competition among those claiming to have seen him first rather than deliberate fraud, while [White](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/L._Michael_White) emphasizes differences in the agendas of the evangelists.[^cite_note-White-401][^cite_note-FOOTNOTESanders1993276–281-458] Differences between accounts were a feature of ancient biographies, such as the accounts of [Otho](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Otho) in Suetonius and Plutarch.[^cite_note-auto-459] Another common hypothesis among historians is that all reported perceptions of Jesus are [confabulated](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Confabulated) or a case of [mistaken identity](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Eyewitness_identification#Causes_of_eyewitness_error).[^cite_note-460] The followers of Jesus formed a community to wait for his return and the founding of his kingdom.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTESanders199311-33]

### Portraits of Jesus

Main articles: [Historical Jesus](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Historical_Jesus) and [Quest for the historical Jesus](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Quest_for_the_historical_Jesus)

Modern research on the historical Jesus has not led to a unified picture of the historical figure, partly because of the variety of academic traditions represented by the scholars.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTETheissenWinter20024–5-461] Given the scarcity of historical sources, it is generally difficult for any scholar to construct a portrait of Jesus that can be considered historically valid beyond the basic elements of his life.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEKöstenbergerKellumQuarles2009117–125-100][^cite_note-FOOTNOTEEhrman199922–23-101] The portraits of Jesus constructed in these quests often differ from each other, and from the image portrayed in the Gospels.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTETheissenWinter20025-344][^cite_note-FOOTNOTECrossLivingstone2005Historical_Jesus,_Quest_of_the-462]

Jesus is seen as the founder of, in the words of Sanders, a "renewal movement within Judaism". One of the criteria used to discern historical details in the "[third quest](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Quest_for_the_historical_Jesus#Third_quest)" is the criterion of plausibility, relative to Jesus's Jewish context and to his influence on Christianity. A disagreement in contemporary research is whether Jesus was [apocalyptic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Apocalypticism). Most scholars conclude that he was an apocalyptic preacher, like [John the Baptist](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/John_the_Baptist) and [Paul the Apostle](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Paul_the_Apostle). Certain prominent North American scholars, such as [Burton Mack](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Burton_Mack) and John Dominic Crossan, advocate for a non-[eschatological](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Eschatological) Jesus, one who is more of a Cynic [sage](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sage_\(philosophy\)) than an apocalyptic preacher.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTETheissenMerz19981–15-463] In addition to portraying Jesus as an apocalyptic prophet, a charismatic healer or a [cynic philosopher](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cynicism_\(philosophy\)), some scholars portray him as the true messiah or an [egalitarian](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Egalitarian) prophet of [social change](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Social_change).[^cite_note-464][^cite_note-FOOTNOTEKöstenbergerKellumQuarles2009124–125-465] The attributes described in the portraits sometimes overlap, and scholars who differ on some attributes sometimes agree on others.[^cite_note-466]

Since the 18th century, scholars have occasionally put forth that Jesus was a political national messiah, but the evidence for this portrait is negligible. Likewise, the proposal that Jesus was a [Zealot](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Zealot) does not fit with the earliest strata of the Synoptic tradition.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTECrossLivingstone2005Jesus_Christ-181]

### Language, ethnicity, and appearance

Further information: [Language of Jesus](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Language_of_Jesus) and [Race and appearance of Jesus](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Race_and_appearance_of_Jesus)

Twelve depictions of Jesus from around the world.

The ethnicity of Jesus in art has been influenced by cultural settings.

[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEHoulden200663–99-467]

[^cite_note-Erricker44-468]

Jesus grew up in Galilee and much of his ministry took place there.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEGreenMcKnightMarshall1992442-469] The languages spoken in Galilee and Judea during the 1st century AD included [Jewish Palestinian Aramaic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jewish_Palestinian_Aramaic), [Hebrew](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hebrew), and [Greek](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Koine_Greek), with Aramaic being predominant.[^cite_note-470][^cite_note-Porter1997-471] There is substantial consensus that Jesus gave most of his teachings in Aramaic[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEDunn2003313–315-472] in the [Galilean dialect](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Galilean_dialect).[^cite_note-473][^cite_note-474] Other than Aramaic and Hebrew, it is likely that he was also able to speak Greek.[^cite_note-Porter1997-471][^cite_note-475][^cite_note-476]

Modern scholars agree that Jesus was a Jew of 1st-century [Judea](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Judea).[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEEhrman199996-477] *[Ioudaios](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ioudaios)* in New Testament Greek[^cite_note-480] is a term which in the contemporary context may refer to religion ([Second Temple Judaism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Second_Temple_Judaism)), ethnicity (of Judea), or both.[^cite_note-481][^cite_note-482][^cite_note-483] In a review of the state of modern scholarship, [Amy-Jill Levine](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Amy-Jill_Levine) writes that the entire question of ethnicity is "fraught with difficulty", and that "beyond recognizing that 'Jesus was Jewish', rarely does the scholarship address what being 'Jewish' means".[^cite_note-FOOTNOTELevine200610-484]

The New Testament gives no description of the physical appearance of Jesus before his death—it is generally indifferent to racial appearances and does not refer to the features of the people it mentions.[^cite_note-485][^cite_note-Perkinson30-486][^cite_note-487] Jesus probably looked like a typical Jewish man of his time and place; standing around 166 cm (5 ft 5 in) tall with a thin but fit build, [olive-brown skin](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Olive_skin), brown eyes and short, dark hair. He also probably had a beard that was not particularly long or heavy.[^cite_note-488]

### Christ myth theory

Main article: [Christ myth theory](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Christ_myth_theory)

The Christ myth theory is the hypothesis that Jesus of Nazareth never existed; or that if he did, he had virtually nothing to do with the founding of Christianity and the accounts in the [gospels](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gospels).[^cite_note-490] Stories of Jesus's birth, along with other key events, have so many mythic elements that some scholars have suggested that Jesus himself was a myth.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTETheissenMerz1998113–115-491]

[Bruno Bauer](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bruno_Bauer) (1809–1882) taught that the first Gospel was a work of literature that produced history rather than described it. According to [Albert Kalthoff](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Albert_Kalthoff) (1850–1906), a social movement produced Jesus when it encountered Jewish messianic expectations. [Arthur Drews](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Arthur_Drews) (1865–1935) saw Jesus as the concrete form of a myth that predated Christianity.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTETheissenMerz199890-492] Despite arguments put forward by authors who have questioned the existence of a [historical Jesus](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Historical_Jesus), virtually all scholars of antiquity accept that Jesus was a historical figure and consider the myth theory to be fringe.[^cite_note-493]

## Religious perspectives

Main article: [Religious perspectives on Jesus](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Religious_perspectives_on_Jesus)

Jesus's teachings and the retelling of his life story have significantly influenced the course of [human history](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Human_history), and have directly or indirectly affected the lives of billions of people, even non-Christians, worldwide.[^cite_note-494][^cite_note-Evans1996-495] He is considered by many people to be the most influential figure to have ever lived, finding a significant place in numerous cultural contexts.[^cite_note-496][^cite_note-497]

Apart from his own disciples and followers,[^cite_note-498] the Jews of Jesus's day generally [rejected him as the messiah](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rejection_of_Jesus),[^cite_note-FOOTNOTELevine200761-499] as does Judaism today.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTELevine200717-500] Christian theologians, [ecumenical councils](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ecumenical_council), reformers and others have written extensively about Jesus over the centuries. [Christian denominations](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Christian_denomination) have often been defined or characterized by their descriptions of Jesus. Meanwhile, [Manichaeans](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Manichaeans), [Gnostics](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gnostics), [Muslims](19541.md), [Druzes](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Druze),[^cite_note-Hitti-501] the [Baháʼís](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bah%C3%A1%CA%BC%C3%ADs), and others have found prominent places for Jesus in their religions.[^cite_note-502][^cite_note-Evans1996-495][^cite_note-503]

### Christianity

Main articles: [Jesus in Christianity](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jesus_in_Christianity), [Christ (title)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Christ_\(title\)), and [Christology](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Christology)



The [Trinity](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Trinity) is the belief in Christianity that God is one God in three persons: [God the Father](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/God_the_Father), [God the Son](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/God_the_Son) ([Jesus](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jesus_in_Christianity)), and [God the Holy Spirit](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/God_the_Holy_Spirit).



Jesus is depicted with the [Alpha and Omega](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Alpha_and_Omega) letters in the [Catacombs of Rome](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Catacombs_of_Rome) from the 4th century.

Jesus is the central figure of Christianity.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEMcGrath20064–6-111] Although Christian views of Jesus vary, it is possible to summarize the key beliefs shared by the major [denominations](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Christian_denomination), as stated in their [catechetical](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Catechism) or [confessional](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Confessionalism_\(religion\)) texts.[^cite_note-504][^cite_note-505][^cite_note-506] Christian views of Jesus are derived from the texts of the [New Testament](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/New_Testament), including the [canonical gospels](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Canonical_gospels) and letters such as the [Pauline epistles](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pauline_epistles) and the [Johannine writings](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Johannine_writings). These documents outline the key Christian beliefs about Jesus, including his divinity, humanity, and earthly life, and that he is the Christ and the Son of God.[^cite_note-507] Despite their many shared beliefs, not all Christian denominations agree on all doctrines, and both [major and minor differences](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/East%E2%80%93West_Schism) on teachings and beliefs have persisted throughout Christianity for centuries.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTECrossLivingstone2005Great_Schism-508]

The New Testament states that the [resurrection of Jesus](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Resurrection_of_Jesus) is the foundation of the Christian faith.[^cite_note-509][^cite_note-510] Christian doctrine holds that through his sacrificial death and resurrection, humans can be [reconciled with God](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Reconciliation_\(theology\)) and are thereby offered [salvation](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Salvation_in_Christianity) and the promise of [eternal life](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Eternal_life_\(Christianity\)).[^cite_note-Oxford_Companion-226] Recalling the words of John the Baptist in the [gospel of John](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gospel_of_John), these doctrines sometimes refer to Jesus as the [Lamb of God](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lamb_of_God), who was crucified to fulfil his role as the servant of God.[^cite_note-511][^cite_note-512] Jesus is thus seen as the [new and last Adam](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/New_Adam), whose obedience contrasts with [Adam's disobedience](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fall_of_man).[^cite_note-513] Christians view Jesus as a role model, whose God-focused life believers are encouraged to imitate.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEMcGrath20064–6-111]

Most branches of Christianity believe that Jesus is both human and the Son of God.<sup>[\[492\]](#cite_note-FOOTNOTEEhrman2014152"There_were_other_wide-ranging_debates_as_well._Was_the_Hebrew_Bible_—_the_Jewish_scriptures_—_part_of_the_revelation_of_the_true_God?_Or_was_it_simply_a_sacred_book_of_the_Jews,_of_no_relevance_for_Christians?_Or_even_more_extreme,_was_it_authored_by_a_lower,_malevolent_deity?_What_about_the_world_we_live_in?_Was_it_the_creation_of_the_one_true_God?_Or_was_it_the_inferior_creation_of_the_God_of_the_Jews_\(who_was_not_the_God_of_the_Christians\)?_Or_was_it_a_cosmic_disaster_and_inherentiy_evil?_The_reason_most_Christians_today_would_have_no_trouble_answering_any_of_these_questions_is_that_one_perspective_from_early_Christianity_emerged_as_triumphant_in_the_debates_over_what_to_believe_and_how_to_live._This_is_the_side_that_insisted_that_there_was_only_one_true_God;_he_had_created_the_world,_called_the_Jews_to_be_his_people,_and_given_them_his_scriptures._The_world_had_been_created_good,_but_it_had_become_corrupt_because_of_sin._Eventually,_though,_God_would_redeem_the_world_and_al)</sup> While there has been [theological debate](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Christological_controversies) over his nature,[^cite_note-516] Trinitarian Christians generally believe that Jesus is the Logos, God's incarnation and [God the Son](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/God_the_Son), both fully divine and fully human. The doctrine of the Trinity is not universally accepted among Christians.[^cite_note-517][^cite_note-518] With the [Reformation](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Reformation), Christians such as [Michael Servetus](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Michael_Servetus) and the [Socinians](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Socinian) started questioning the ancient creeds that had established Jesus's two natures.[^cite_note-Britannica-40] Nontrinitarian Christian groups include [the Church of Jesus Christ of Latter-day Saints](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/The_Church_of_Jesus_Christ_of_Latter-day_Saints),[^cite_note-519] [Unitarians](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Unitarianism) and [Jehovah's Witnesses](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jehovah's_Witnesses).[^cite_note-FOOTNOTECrossLivingstone2005Antitrinitarianism-515]

Christians revere not only Jesus but also [his name](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Name_of_Jesus). Devotions to the [Holy Name of Jesus](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Holy_Name_of_Jesus) go back to the earliest days of Christianity.[^cite_note-520][^cite_note-FOOTNOTEHoulden2006426-521] These devotions and feasts exist in both [Eastern](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Eastern_Christianity) and [Western Christianity](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Western_Christianity).[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEHoulden2006426-521]

### Judaism

Main article: [Judaism's view of Jesus](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Judaism's_view_of_Jesus)

See also: [Jesus in the Talmud](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jesus_in_the_Talmud)

Judaism rejects the idea of Jesus (or any future Jewish messiah) being God,[^cite_note-JE1906-455] or a mediator to God, or part of a Trinity.[^cite_note-522] It holds that Jesus is not the messiah, arguing that he neither fulfilled the messianic prophecies in the [Tanakh](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tanakh) nor embodied the personal qualifications of the Messiah.[^cite_note-523] Jews argue that Jesus did not fulfil prophecies to build the [Third Temple](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Third_Temple),[^cite_note-524] gather Jews back to Israel,[^cite_note-525] bring world peace,[^cite_note-526] and unite humanity under the God of Israel.[^cite_note-527][^cite_note-528] Furthermore, according to Jewish tradition, there were no prophets after [Malachi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Malachi),[^cite_note-529] who delivered his prophecies in the 5th century BC.[^cite_note-530]

Judaic criticism of Jesus is long-standing and includes a [range of stories](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jesus_in_the_Talmud) found in the [Talmud](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Talmud), written and compiled between the 3rd and 5th centuries.[^cite_note-531] In one such story, *[Yeshu](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Yeshu) HaNozri* ('Jesus the Nazarene'), a lewd apostate, is executed by the Jewish high court for spreading idolatry and practising magic.[^cite_note-532] According to some, the form *Yeshu* is an [acronym](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Acronym) which in Hebrew reads "may his name and memory be blotted out".[^cite_note-533] The majority of contemporary scholars consider that this material provides no information on the historical Jesus.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTETheissenMerz199874–75-534] The *[Mishneh Torah](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mishneh_Torah)*, a late 12th-century work of [Jewish law](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jewish_law) written by [Moses Maimonides](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Moses_Maimonides), states that Jesus is a "stumbling block" who makes "the majority of the world to err and serve a god other than the Lord".[^cite_note-535]

Mediaeval Hebrew literature contains the anecdotal "Episode of Jesus" (known also as *[Toledot Yeshu](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Toledot_Yeshu)*), in which Jesus is described as being the son of Joseph, the son of [Pandera](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tiberius_Julius_Abdes_Pantera) (see: [*Episode of Jesus*](https://en.wikisource.org/wiki/Translation\:Story%20of%20Jesus)). The account portrays Jesus as an imposter.[^cite_note-536]

### Manichaeism

Main article: [Jesus in Manichaeism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jesus_in_Manichaeism)

[Manichaeism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Manichaeism), an ancient religious movement, became one of the earliest organized religions outside of Christianity to honour Jesus as a significant figure.[^cite_note-537][^cite_note-538][^cite_note-539] Within the Manichaean belief system, Jesus is revered alongside other prominent prophets such as [Zoroaster](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Zoroaster), [Gautama Buddha](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gautama_Buddha), and [Mani](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mani_\(prophet\)) himself.[^cite_note-540][^cite_note-541]

### Islam

A major figure in Islam,[^cite_note-542][^cite_note-543][^cite_note-CEI-544] Jesus (often referred to by his Quranic name *[*ʿĪsā*](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Isa_\(name\))*)[^cite_note-547]  is considered to be a [messenger](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Prophets_and_messengers_in_Islam) of [God](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/God_in_Islam) and the messiah ([*al-Masīḥ*](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Masih_\(title\))) who was sent to guide the [Children of Israel](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Israelites) (*Banī Isrāʾīl*) with a new scripture, the Gospel (referred to in Islam as [*Injīl*](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gospel_in_Islam)).[^cite_note-CEI-544][^cite_note-548] The form *ʿĪsē* is a pre-Islamic [phonosemantic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Phono-semantic_matching) correspondence with the [Safaitic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Safaitic) name *ʿsy*, attested in Arabian inscriptions.[^cite_note-Al-Jallad-2021-546] Muslims regard the gospels' accounts in the New Testament as partially authentic, and believe that Jesus's original message was altered (*[taḥrīf](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ta%E1%B8%A5r%C4%ABf)*) and that [Muhammad](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Muhammad_in_Islam) came later to revive it.[^cite_note-549] Belief in Jesus (and all other [messengers of God](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Prophets_in_Islam)) is a requirement for being a [Muslim](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Muslim).[^cite_note-550] The Quran mentions Jesus by name 25 times—more often than Muhammad[^cite_note-551][^cite_note-552]—and emphasizes that Jesus was a mortal human who, like all other prophets, had been divinely chosen to spread God's message.[^cite_note-comparative-553] While the Quran affirms the Virgin birth of Jesus, he is considered to be neither an incarnation nor the [son of God](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Son_of_God_\(Christianity\)).[^cite_note-554][^cite_note-Morgan-555] Islamic texts emphasize a strict notion of [monotheism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Monotheism) (*[tawḥīd](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Taw%E1%B8%A5%C4%ABd)*) and forbid the association of partners with God, which would be [idolatry](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Shirk_\(Islam\)).[^cite_note-556]



Islamic [miniature](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Persian_miniature) of Maryam in labour shaking a date palm, with baby Isa and hidden voice below



Persian miniature depicting a man who outwardly appeared to the Jews as Jesus being hanged

The Quran describes the annunciation to Mary ([*Maryam*](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mary_in_Islam)) by the Holy Spirit that she is to give birth to Jesus while remaining a virgin. It calls the virgin birth a miracle that occurred by the will of God.[^cite_note-RobB32-557][^cite_note-Peters23-558] The Quran ([21\:91](https://quran.com/21?startingVerse=91) and [66\:12](https://quran.com/66?startingVerse=12)) states that God breathed [his spirit](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Holy_Spirit_\(Islam\)) into Mary while she was chaste.[^cite_note-RobB32-557][^cite_note-Peters23-558] Jesus is called a "spirit from God" because he was born through the action of the Spirit,[^cite_note-RobB32-557] but that belief does not imply [his pre-existence](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pre-existence_of_Christ).[^cite_note-559]

To aid in his ministry to the Jewish people, Jesus was given the ability to perform miracles, by permission of God rather than by his own power.[^cite_note-Morgan-555] Through his ministry, Jesus is seen as a [precursor](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Precursor_\(religion\)) to Muhammad.[^cite_note-comparative-553] In the Quran ([4\:155–160](https://quran.com/4?startingVerse=155)) it is said that the Jews claim to have killed Jesus in a similar manner to previous prophets sent to them, but that this was only made to appear that way, and that they neither killed nor crucified him and that he was raised into the heavens by God and that he will testify against the Jews during the [Day of Resurrection](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Judgement_Day_in_Islam).[^cite_note-560]



16th-century Ottoman manuscript illustration depicting Isa ascending to heaven, carried by two angels; below him, the Jews who sought to kill him stand astonished in disbelief.

According to most classic [Sunni](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sunni) and [Twelver Shi'ite](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Twelver_Shi'ite) interpretations of these verses, the likeness of Jesus was cast upon a [substitute](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Substitution_hypothesis) (most often one of his disciples), who was crucified in Jesus's stead.[^cite_note-561] Some mediaeval Muslims, including the [*ghulāt*](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ghulat) writing under the name of [al-Mufaddal ibn Umar al-Ju'fi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Al-Mufaddal_ibn_Umar_al-Ju'fi), the [Brethren of Purity](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Brethren_of_Purity), various [Isma'ili](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Isma'ili) philosophers, and the Sunni mystic [al-Ghazali](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Al-Ghazali), affirmed the historicity of Jesus's crucifixion. These thinkers held the [docetic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Docetic) view that, although Jesus's human body had died on the cross, his spirit had survived and ascended into heaven, so that his death was only an appearance.[^cite_note-562] Nevertheless, to Muslims it is the ascension rather than the crucifixion that constitutes a major event in the life of Jesus.[^cite_note-563] There is no mention of his resurrection on the third day, and his death plays no special role in [Islamic theories of salvation](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Soteriology#Islam).[^cite_note-564] Jesus is a central figure in [Islamic eschatology](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Islamic_eschatology): Muslims believe that [he will return to Earth](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Second_Coming#Islam) at the [end of time](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Eschatology) and defeat the [Antichrist](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Antichrist) (*[ad-Dajjal](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ad-Dajjal)*) by killing him.[^cite_note-CEI-544][^cite_note-565][^cite_note-FOOTNOTEGrudem1994568–603-566][^cite_note-567]

According to the Quran, the coming of [Muhammad](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Muhammad) (also called "Ahmad") was predicted by Jesus:

> And ˹remember˺ when Jesus, son of Mary, said, "O children of Israel! I am truly Allah's messenger to you, confirming the Torah which came before me, and giving good news of a messenger after me whose name will be Aḥmad." Yet when the Prophet came to them with clear proofs, they said, "This is pure magic."

— [Surah As-Saf](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/As-Saff) [61\:6](https://quran.com/61?startingVerse=6)

Through this verse, early Arab Muslims claimed legitimacy for their new faith in the existing religious traditions and the predictions of Jesus.[^cite_note-568]

#### Ahmadiyya

Main article: [Jesus in Ahmadiyya](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jesus_in_Ahmadiyya)

The [Ahmadiyya](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ahmadiyya) Muslim Community has [several teachings](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jesus_in_Ahmadiyya_Islam) about Jesus.[^cite_note-569] Ahmadis believe that he was a mortal man who survived his crucifixion and died a natural death at the age of 120 in [Kashmir](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kashmir), India, and is buried at [Roza Bal](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Roza_Bal).[^cite_note-570]

### Druze

Further information: [Religious perspectives on Jesus § Druze](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Religious_perspectives_on_Jesus#Druze)

In the [Druze faith](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Druze_faith),[^cite_note-Hitti-501] Jesus is considered and revered as one of the seven spokesmen or prophets (*natiq*), defined as messengers or intermediaries between God and mankind, along with figures including [Moses](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Moses), [Muhammad](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Muhammad) and [Muhammad ibn Isma'il](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Muhammad_ibn_Isma'il), each of them sent at a different period of history to preach the message of God.[^cite_note-Hitti-501][^cite_note-571][^cite_note-572][^cite_note-573][^cite_note-574] In Druze tradition, Jesus is known under three titles: the True Messiah (*al-Masih al-Haq*), the Messiah of all Nations (*Masih al-Umam*), and the Messiah of Sinners. This is due, respectively, to the belief that Jesus delivered the true Gospel message, the belief that he was the Saviour of all nations, and the belief that he offers forgiveness.[^cite_note-575]

### Baháʼí Faith

In the [Baháʼí Faith](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bah%C3%A1%CA%BC%C3%AD_Faith), Jesus is considered one of the [Manifestations of God](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Manifestation_of_God_\(Bah%C3%A1%CA%BC%C3%AD_Faith\)),[^cite_note-576] defined as divine messengers or prophets sent by God to guide humanity, along with other religious figures such as Moses, [Krishna](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Krishna), [Zoroaster](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Zoroaster), [Buddha](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Buddha), Muhammad, and [Baháʼu'lláh](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bah%C3%A1%CA%BCu'll%C3%A1h). Baháʼís believe that these religious founders or leaders have contributed to the [progressive revelation](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Progressive_revelation_\(Bah%C3%A1%CA%BC%C3%AD\)) by bringing spiritual and moral values to humanity in their own time and place.[^cite_note-577][^cite_note-578][^cite_note-579][^cite_note-580][^cite_note-581] As a Manifestation of God, Jesus is believed to reflect God's qualities and attributes, but is not considered the only saviour of humanity nor the incarnation of God.[^cite_note-582][^cite_note-583][^cite_note-584] Baháʼís believe in the virgin birth,[^cite_note-585][^cite_note-Cole-586] but see the resurrection and the miracles of Jesus as symbolic.[^cite_note-587][^cite_note-Cole-586]

### Other

See also: [Criticism of Jesus](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Criticism_of_Jesus)



Jesus depicted as the liberator of Black slaves, on the masthead of the [abolitionist](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Abolitionism_in_the_United_States) paper *[The Liberator](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/The_Liberator_\(newspaper\))*



Enthroned Jesus image on a [Manichaean](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Manichaeism) temple banner from c. 10th-century [Qocho](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Qocho)

In [Christian Gnosticism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Christian_Gnosticism) (now a largely extinct religious movement),[^cite_note-588] Jesus was sent from the [divine realm](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pleroma#Gnosticism) and provided the secret knowledge ([gnosis](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gnosis)) necessary for salvation. It is important to note that Gnosticism is not a homogeneous religion, but an umbrella term used by modern scholars to describe diverse religious and philosophical ideas and systems that emerged in the late first century among [early Christian](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Early_Christianity) sects and other religious movements.[^cite_note-589] Most Gnostics believed that Jesus was a human who became possessed by the spirit of "the Christ" at his baptism. This spirit left Jesus's body during the crucifixion but was rejoined to him when he was raised from the dead. Some Gnostics were [docetics](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Docetics), believing that Jesus did not have a physical body, but only appeared to possess one.[^cite_note-590] The Gnostic Jesus can both differ greatly from the Christian Jesus, but also build on him. For instance, the [Testimony of Truth](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Testimony_of_Truth), a Gnostic Christian text found in the [Nag Hammadi library](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nag_Hammadi_library) buried around 400 AD, explains that the [serpent](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Serpents_in_the_Bible) in Genesis 3 who instructs [Adam](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Adam) and [Eve](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Eve) is Jesus.[^cite_note-591]

Hinduism has no established set of beliefs and no universal or common view of Jesus. However, a lot of Hindus, including religious and political leaders, tend to variously consider Jesus as either a [Āchārya](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Acharya), [Sadhu](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sadhu) or [Avatar](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Avatar).[^cite_note-592][^cite_note-593][^cite_note-594] Some [Hare Krishnas](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/International_Society_for_Krishna_Consciousness) also claim that Jesus Christ is an exemplary Hare Krishna [devotee](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hindu_devotional_movements), and was [predicted](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Prediction) and [prophesied](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Prophecy) in the [Bhavishya Purana](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bhavishya_Purana).[^cite_note-595]

[Paramahansa Yogananda](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Paramahansa_Yogananda), an Indian [guru](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Guru), taught that Jesus was the reincarnation of [Elisha](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Elisha) and a student of John the Baptist, the reincarnation of [Elijah](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Elijah).[^cite_note-596] Some [Buddhists](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Buddhist), including [Tenzin Gyatso, the 14th Dalai Lama](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tenzin_Gyatso,_the_14th_Dalai_Lama), regard Jesus as a [bodhisattva](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bodhisattva) who dedicated his life to the welfare of people.[^cite_note-597] The [New Age](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/New_Age) movement entertains a wide variety of views on Jesus.[^cite_note-598]

[Theosophists](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Theosophists), from whom many New Age teachings originated,[^cite_note-599] refer to Jesus as the [Master Jesus](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Master_Jesus), a spiritual reformer, and they believe that Christ, after [various incarnations](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Reincarnation), occupied the body of Jesus.[^cite_note-600] In the [Anthroposophy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Anthroposophy) founded by [Rudolf Steiner](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rudolf_Steiner), Jesus Christ is a central balancing force mediating between the two opposing [polarities](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dualism) of [evil](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Evil), namely the fanatical exalted [mysticism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mysticism) of [Lucifer](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lucifer), and the cold [materialism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Materialism) of [Ahriman](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ahriman).[^cite_note-601]

[Atheists](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Atheist) reject Jesus's divinity, but have different views about him—from challenging [his mental health](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mental_health_of_Jesus)[^cite_note-602][^cite_note-603] to emphasizing his "moral superiority" ([Richard Dawkins](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Richard_Dawkins)).[^cite_note-604]

## Artistic depictions

Main articles: [Depiction of Jesus](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Depiction_of_Jesus) and [Life of Christ in art](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Life_of_Christ_in_art)

An ancient wall painting depicting Jesus

Jesus healing a paralytic in one of the first known images of Jesus from [Dura Europos](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dura-Europos_church) in the 3rd century

[^cite_note-605]

As in other [Early Christian art](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Early_Christian_art), the earliest depictions date to the late 2nd or early 3rd century, and surviving images are found in the [Catacombs of Rome](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Catacombs_of_Rome).[^cite_note-606] Some of the earliest depictions of Jesus at the [Dura-Europos church](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dura-Europos_church) date to before 256.[^cite_note-607] A wide range of depictions of Jesus appeared during the next two millennia, influenced by cultural settings, political circumstances and theological contexts.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEHoulden200663–99-467][^cite_note-Erricker44-468][^cite_note-Perkinson30-486]

The depiction of Christ in pictorial form was highly [controversial](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Aniconism_in_Christianity) in the early Church.[^cite_note-608][^cite_note-609][^cite_note-610] From the 5th century, flat painted [icons](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Icon) became popular in the Eastern Church.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTECrossLivingstone2005Icons-611] The [Byzantine Iconoclasm](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Byzantine_Iconoclasm) acted as a barrier to developments in the East, but by the 9th century, art was permitted again.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEHoulden200663–99-467] The [Protestant Reformation](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Protestant_Reformation) brought renewed resistance to imagery, but total prohibition was atypical, and Protestant objections to images have tended to reduce since the 16th century. Although large images are generally avoided, few Protestants now object to book illustrations depicting Jesus.[^cite_note-612][^cite_note-613] The use of depictions of Jesus is advocated by the leaders of denominations such as [Anglicans](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Anglicans) and [Catholics](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Catholics)[^cite_note-614][^cite_note-615][^cite_note-616] and is a key element of the [Eastern Orthodox](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Eastern_Orthodox) tradition.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEDoninger1999231-617][^cite_note-618]

In Eastern Christian art, the [Transfiguration](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Transfiguration_of_Jesus_in_Christian_art) was a major theme, with every Eastern Orthodox monk trained in icon painting having to prove his craft by painting an icon depicting it.[^cite_note-619] Icons receive the external marks of veneration, such as kisses and prostration, and they are thought to be powerful channels of divine grace.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTECrossLivingstone2005Icons-611]

In Western Europe, the [Renaissance](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Renaissance) brought forth artists who focused on depictions of Jesus; [Fra Angelico](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fra_Angelico) and others followed [Giotto](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Giotto) in the systematic development of uncluttered images.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEHoulden200663–99-467] Before the Protestant Reformation, the [crucifix](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Crucifix) was common in Western Christianity. It is a model of the cross with Jesus crucified on it. The crucifix became the central ornament of the altar in the 13th century, a use that has been nearly universal in Roman Catholic churches since then.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTECrossLivingstone2005Crucifix-620]

## Associated relics

Main article: [Relics associated with Jesus](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Relics_associated_with_Jesus)



The [Shroud of Turin](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Shroud_of_Turin), Italy, is the best-known claimed relic of Jesus and one of the most studied artefacts in human history.

[^cite_note-NatureMaterials-621]

The total destruction that ensued with the [siege of Jerusalem](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Siege_of_Jerusalem_\(70_AD\)) by the Romans in AD 70 made the survival of items from 1st-century Judea very rare, and almost no direct records survive about the history of Judaism from the last part of the 1st century to the 2nd century.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTELevine200624–25-622][^cite_note-Koester382-623][^cite_note-625] Biblical scholar [Margaret M. Mitchell](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Margaret_M._Mitchell) writes that, although [Eusebius](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Eusebius) (4th century) reports (*[Ecclesiastical History](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Church_History_\(Eusebius\))* III 5.3) that the early Christians left Jerusalem for [Pella](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pella,_Jordan) just before Jerusalem was subjected to the final lockdown, we must accept that no items from the early Jerusalem Church have survived.[^cite_note-626] Paranormal investigator [Joe Nickell](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Joe_Nickell) writes, "as investigation after investigation has shown, not a single, reliably authenticated relic of Jesus exists."[^cite_note-627][^cite_note-629]

Throughout the history of Christianity, [relics](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Relic) attributed to Jesus have been claimed, but doubt has been cast on them. The 16th-century Catholic theologian [Erasmus](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Erasmus) wrote sarcastically about the proliferation of relics and the number of buildings that could have been constructed from the wood claimed to be from the [cross used in the Crucifixion](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/True_Cross).[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEDillenberger19995-630] Similarly, while experts debate whether Jesus was crucified with three nails or four, at least thirty [holy nails](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Holy_nail) are venerated as relics across Europe.[^cite_note-631]

Some relics, such as purported remnants of the [crown of thorns](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Crown_of_thorns) placed on the head of Jesus, receive only a modest number of [pilgrims](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Christian_pilgrimage), while the [Shroud of Turin](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Shroud_of_Turin) (which is associated with an approved [Catholic devotion](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Catholic_devotion) to the [Holy Face of Jesus](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Holy_Face_of_Jesus)), has received millions,[^cite_note-632] including the popes [John Paul II](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/John_Paul_II) and [Benedict XVI](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Benedict_XVI).[^cite_note-633][^cite_note-634]

[^cite_note-3]: &#x20;The facial asymmetry is deliberate.<sup>[\[1\]](#cite_note-1)</sup><sup>[\[2\]](#cite_note-2)</sup>

[^cite_note-8]: &#x20;[John P. Meier](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/John_P._Meier) writes that Jesus's birth year is c. 7 or 6 BC.<sup>[\[3\]](#cite_note-FOOTNOTEMeier1991407-4)</sup> [Karl Rahner](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Karl_Rahner) states that the consensus among Christian scholars is c. 4 BC.<sup>[\[4\]](#cite_note-FOOTNOTERahner2004732-5)</sup> [E. P. Sanders](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/E._P._Sanders) also favours c. 4 BC and refers to the general consensus.<sup>[\[5\]](#cite_note-FOOTNOTESanders199310–11-6)</sup> [Jack Finegan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jack_Finegan) uses the study of early Christian traditions to support c. 3 or 2 BC.<sup>[\[6\]](#cite_note-Finegan-7)</sup>

[^cite_note-14]: &#x20;[James Dunn](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/James_Dunn_\(theologian\)) writes that the baptism and crucifixion of Jesus "command almost universal assent" and "rank so high on the 'almost impossible to doubt or deny' scale of historical facts" that they are often the starting points for the study of the historical Jesus.<sup>[\[8\]](#cite_note-FOOTNOTEDunn2003339-10)</sup> [Bart D. Ehrman](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bart_D._Ehrman) states that the crucifixion of Jesus on the orders of Pontius Pilate is the most certain element about him.<sup>[\[9\]](#cite_note-FOOTNOTEEhrman1999101-11)</sup> [John Dominic Crossan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/John_Dominic_Crossan) and Richard G. Watts state that the crucifixion of Jesus is as certain as any historical fact can be.<sup>[\[10\]](#cite_note-FOOTNOTECrossanWatts199996-12)</sup> Paul R. Eddy and [Gregory A. Boyd](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gregory_A._Boyd) say that non-Christian confirmation of the crucifixion of Jesus is now "firmly established".<sup>[\[11\]](#cite_note-FOOTNOTEEddyBoyd2007173-13)</sup>

[^cite_note-15]: &#x20;Traditionally, Christians believe that Mary conceived her son miraculously by the agency of the Holy Spirit. Muslims believe that she conceived her son miraculously by the command of God. Joseph was from these perspectives and according to the canonical gospels the acting adoptive father of Jesus.

[^cite_note-16]: &#x20;[Ancient Greek](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ancient_Greek_language): Ἰησοῦς, romanized: *Iēsoús*, probably from [Hebrew](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hebrew) or [Aramaic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Aramaic): יֵשׁוּעַ, romanized: *[Yēšūaʿ](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Yeshua)*

[^cite_note-17]: &#x20;[Coptic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Coptic_language): Ⲓⲏⲥⲟⲩⲥ Ⲡⲓⲭ́ρⲓⲥτⲟⲥ; [Geʽez](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ge%CA%BDez): መሲህ ኢየሱስ; [Greek](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Koine_Greek): Ἰησοῦς Χριστός; [Hebrew](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Classical_Hebrew): ישוע המשיח; [Latin](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Latin): *Iesus Christus*; [Slavonic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Church_Slavonic): І҆исоу́съ Хрїсто́съ; [Syriac](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Classical_Syriac): ܝܫܘܥ ܡܫܺܝܚܳܐ

[^cite_note-22]: &#x20;Ehrman writes: "The notion that the Gospel accounts are not completely accurate but still important for the religious truths they try to convey is widely shared in the scholarly world, even though it's not so widely known or believed outside of it."<sup>[\[14\]](#cite_note-20)</sup>\
    Sanders writes: "The earliest Christians did not write a narrative of Jesus' life, but rather made use of, and thus preserved, individual units—short passages about his words and deeds. These units were later moved and arranged by authors and editors. \[...] Some material has been revised and some created by early Christians."<sup>[\[15\]](#cite_note-FOOTNOTESanders199357-21)</sup>

[^cite_note-exist-32]: &#x20;In a 2011 review of the state of modern scholarship, [Bart D. Ehrman](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bart_D._Ehrman) wrote, "He certainly existed, as virtually every competent scholar of antiquity, Christian or non-Christian, agrees."<sup>[\[18\]](#cite_note-FOOTNOTEEhrman2011\[httpsarchiveorgdetailsforged_ehrm_2011_000_10544376pagen298_285]-25)</sup> [Richard A. Burridge](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Richard_A._Burridge) states: "There are those who argue that Jesus is a figment of the Church's imagination, that there never was a Jesus at all. I have to say that I do not know any respectable critical scholar who says that any more."<sup>[\[19\]](#cite_note-26)</sup> [Robert M. Price](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Robert_M._Price) does not believe that Jesus existed but agrees that this perspective runs against the views of the majority of scholars.<sup>[\[20\]](#cite_note-27)</sup> [James D. G. Dunn](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/James_D._G._Dunn) calls the theories of Jesus's non-existence "a thoroughly dead thesis".<sup>[\[21\]](#cite_note-28)</sup> According to [Michael Grant](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Michael_Grant_\(author\)) (a [classicist](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Classicist)), "In recent years \[as of 2004], 'no serious scholar has ventured to postulate the non historicity of Jesus' or at any rate very few, and they have not succeeded in disposing of the much stronger, indeed very abundant, evidence to the contrary."<sup>[\[22\]](#cite_note-29)</sup> [Robert E. Van Voorst](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Robert_E._Van_Voorst) states that biblical scholars and classical historians regard theories of non-existence of Jesus as effectively refuted.<sup>[\[23\]](#cite_note-FOOTNOTEVan_Voorst200016-30)</sup> Writing on *[The Daily Beast](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/The_Daily_Beast)*, [Candida Moss](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Candida_Moss) and Joel Baden state that, "there is nigh universal consensus among biblical scholars – the authentic ones, at least – that Jesus was, in fact, a real guy."<sup>[\[24\]](#cite_note-31)</sup>

[^cite_note-35]: &#x20;A small minority of Christian denominations reject trinitarianism, wholly or partly, as non-scriptural.

[^cite_note-36]: &#x20;Most Christians and [Alawites](436616.md) celebrate Christmas. Part of the [Eastern Christian](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Eastern_Christian) churches celebrate Christmas on 25 December of the [Julian calendar](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Julian_calendar), which currently corresponds to 7 January in the [Gregorian calendar](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gregorian_calendar). Christmas celebrations begin on [Christmas Eve](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Christmas_Eve), generally on 24 December.

[^cite_note-124]: &#x20;Compare [Matthew 1\:6–16](https://www.biblica.com/bible/?osis=niv\:Matthew%201\:6%E2%80%9316) with [Luke 3\:23–31](https://www.biblica.com/bible/?osis=niv\:Luke%203\:23%E2%80%9331). See also [Genealogy of Jesus § Comparison of the two genealogies](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Genealogy_of_Jesus#Comparison_of_the_two_genealogies).

[^cite_note-291]: &#x20;Matthew: "claiming to be king of the Jews". Mark: "King of the Jews". Luke: "subverting nation, opposing payment of taxes to Caesar, claiming to be Christ, a king" John: "breaking Jewish law, claiming to be the son of God".

[^cite_note-343]: &#x20;[Amy-Jill Levine](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Amy-Jill_Levine) writes: "There is a consensus of sorts on a basic outline of Jesus' life. Most scholars agree that Jesus was baptized by John, debated with fellow Jews on how best to live according to God's will, engaged in healings and exorcisms, taught in parables, gathered male and female followers in Galilee, went to Jerusalem, and was crucified by Roman soldiers during the governorship of Pontius Pilate."<sup>[\[325\]](#cite_note-342)</sup>

[^cite_note-370]: &#x20;Tuckett writes: "All this does at least render highly implausible any far-fetched theories that even Jesus' very existence was a Christian invention. The fact that Jesus existed, that he was crucified under Pontius Pilate (for whatever reason) and that he had a band of followers who continued to support his cause, seems to be part of the bedrock of historical tradition. If nothing else, the non-Christian evidence can provide us with certainty on that score."<sup>[\[351\]](#cite_note-369)</sup>

[^cite_note-386]: &#x20;For example, John P. Meier states that Jesus's birth year is c. 7/6 BC,<sup>[\[3\]](#cite_note-FOOTNOTEMeier1991407-4)</sup> while Finegan favours c. 3/2 BC.<sup>[\[6\]](#cite_note-Finegan-7)</sup>

[^cite_note-480]: &#x20;In the New Testament, Jesus is described as Jewish / Judean (*Ioudaios* as written in Koine Greek) on three occasions: by the Magi in [Matthew 2\:2](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Matthew_2\:2), who referred to Jesus as "King of the Jews" (*basileus ton ioudaion*); by both the [Samaritan woman at the well](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Samaritan_woman_at_the_well) in [John 4\:9](https://www.biblegateway.com/passage/?search=John+4\:9\&version=nkjv) and [John 4\:20](https://www.biblegateway.com/passage/?search=John+4\:20\&version=nkjv) and by Jesus himself in [John 4\:22](https://www.biblegateway.com/passage/?search=John+4\:22\&version=nkjv); and (in all four gospels) during the Passion, by the Romans, who also used the phrase "King of the Jews".<sup>[\[458\]](#cite_note-478)</sup> Jesus was also described as "King of Israel" in [John 1\:49](https://www.biblegateway.com/passage/?search=John+1\:49\&version=nkjv), [John 12\:13](https://www.biblegateway.com/passage/?search=John+12\:13\&version=nkjv), [Mark 15\:32](https://www.biblegateway.com/passage/?search=Mark+15\:32\&version=nkjv) and [Matthew 27\:42](https://www.biblegateway.com/passage/?search=Matthew+27\:42\&version=nkjv).<sup>[\[459\]](#cite_note-479)</sup>

[^cite_note-490]: &#x20;Ehrman writes: "In simpler terms, the historical Jesus did not exist. Or if he did, he had virtually nothing to do with the founding of Christianity." Further quoting as authoritative the fuller definition provided by [Earl Doherty](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Earl_Doherty) in *Jesus: Neither God Nor Man*. Age of Reason, 2009, pp. vii–viii: it is "the theory that no historical Jesus worthy of the name existed, that Christianity began with a belief in a spiritual, mythical figure, that the Gospels are essentially allegory and fiction, and that no single identifiable person lay at the root of the Galilean preaching tradition".<sup>[\[468\]](#cite_note-FOOTNOTEEhrman201212-489)</sup>

[^cite_note-516]: &#x20;Following the [Apostolic Age](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Apostolic_Age), there was fierce and often politicized debate in the [early church](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Early_church) on many interrelated issues. [Christology](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Christology) was a major focus of these debates, and was addressed at every one of the [first seven ecumenical councils](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/First_seven_ecumenical_councils). Some early beliefs viewed Jesus as ontologically subordinate to the Father ([Subordinationism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Subordinationism)), and others considered him an aspect of the Father rather than a separate person ([Sabellianism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sabellianism)), both were condemned as heresies by the Catholic Church.<sup>[\[28\]](#cite_note-Britannica-40)</sup><sup>[\[493\]](#cite_note-FOOTNOTECrossLivingstone2005Antitrinitarianism-515)</sup> The Church resolved the issues in ancient councils, which established the Holy Trinity, with Jesus both fully human and fully God.<sup>[\[28\]](#cite_note-Britannica-40)</sup>

[^cite_note-547]: &#x20;In [Qira'at](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Qira'at) (Quranic recitations), the name is pronounced with varying degrees of [vowel inclination](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Imala):<sup>[\[522\]](#cite_note-545)</sup><sup>[\[523\]](#cite_note-Al-Jallad-2021-546)</sup> [\[/ʕiːsæː/\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Help\:IPA/Arabic): Recited by [Warsh](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Warsh) (via the al-Azraq path). [\[/ʕiːseː/\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Help\:IPA/Arabic): Recited by [Hamzah](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hamzah_az-Zaiyyat) and [al-Kisa'i](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Al-Kisa'i). [\[/ʕiːsaː/\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Help\:IPA/Arabic): Recited by the remaining canonical readers.

AD 75) in *[The Jewish War](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/The_Jewish_War)* (Book VII 1.1) stated that Jerusalem had been flattened to the point that "there was left nothing to make those that came thither believe it had ever been inhabited".<sup>[\[599\]](#cite_note-624)</sup> And once what was left of the ruins of Jerusalem had been turned into the Roman settlement of [Aelia Capitolina](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Aelia_Capitolina), no Jews were allowed to set foot in it.<sup>[\[598\]](#cite_note-Koester382-623)</sup>

[^cite_note-332]: &#x20;[Catholic Encyclopedia: Proselyte](https://www.newadvent.org/cathen/12481c.htm) [Archived](https://web.archive.org/web/20170610204154/https://www.newadvent.org/cathen/12481c.htm) 10 June 2017 at the [Wayback Machine](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Wayback_Machine): "The English term 'proselyte' occurs only in the New Testament where it signifies a convert to the Jewish religion ([Matthew 23\:15](https://bible.usccb.org/bible/Matthew/23?15); [Acts 2\:11](https://bible.usccb.org/bible/Acts/2?11); [6\:5](https://bible.usccb.org/bible/Acts/6?5); etc.), though the same Greek word is commonly used in the [Septuagint](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Septuagint) to designate a foreigner living in Palestine. Thus the term seems to have passed from an original local and chiefly political sense, in which it was used as early as 300 BC, to a technical and religious meaning in the Judaism of the [New Testament epoch](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Apostolic_Age)."

[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEEhrman2014152"There_were_other_wide-ranging_debates_as_well._Was_the_Hebrew_Bible_—_the_Jewish_scriptures_—_part_of_the_revelation_of_the_true_God?_Or_was_it_simply_a_sacred_book_of_the_Jews,_of_no_relevance_for_Christians?_Or_even_more_extreme,_was_it_authored_by_a_lower,_malevolent_deity?_What_about_the_world_we_live_in?_Was_it_the_creation_of_the_one_true_God?_Or_was_it_the_inferior_creation_of_the_God_of_the_Jews_(who_was_not_the_God_of_the_Christians)?_Or_was_it_a_cosmic_disaster_and_inherentiy_evil?_The_reason_most_Christians_today_would_have_no_trouble_answering_any_of_these_questions_is_that_one_perspective_from_early_Christianity_emerged_as_triumphant_in_the_debates_over_what_to_believe_and_how_to_live._This_is_the_side_that_insisted_that_there_was_only_one_true_God;_he_had_created_the_world,_called_the_Jews_to_be_his_people,_and_given_them_his_scriptures._The_world_had_been_created_good,_but_it_had_become_corrupt_because_of_sin._Eventually,_though,_God_would_redeem_the_world_and_all_of_his_true_followers_in_it._This_redemption_would_come_through_his_Son,_Jesus_Christ,_who_was_both_God_and_human_at_one_and_the_same_time,_the_one_who_died_for_the_salvation_of_all_who_believe_in_him."-514]: &#x20;[Ehrman 2014](#CITEREFEhrman2014), p. 152, "There were other wide-ranging debates as well. Was the Hebrew Bible — the Jewish scriptures — part of the revelation of the true God? Or was it simply a sacred book of the Jews, of no relevance for Christians? Or even more extreme, was it authored by a lower, malevolent deity? What about the world we live in? Was it the creation of the one true God? Or was it the inferior creation of the God of the Jews (who was not the God of the Christians)? Or was it a cosmic disaster and inherentiy evil? The reason most Christians today would have no trouble answering any of these questions is that one perspective from early Christianity emerged as triumphant in the debates over what to believe and how to live. This is the side that insisted that there was only one true God; he had created the world, called the Jews to be his people, and given them his scriptures. The world had been created good, but it had become corrupt because of sin. Eventually, though, God would redeem the world and all of his true followers in it. This redemption would come through his Son, Jesus Christ, who was both God and human at one and the same time, the one who died for the salvation of all who believe in him.".

[^cite_note-561]: &#x20;[Robinson 2005](#CITEREFRobinson2005); [Lawson 2009](#CITEREFLawson2009). The substitution theory was criticized and rejected by the Sunni Quran commentator [Fakhr al-Din al-Razi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fakhr_al-Din_al-Razi) (1150–1210); see [Lawson 2009](#CITEREFLawson2009), pp. 156–162. According to [Abu Mansur al-Baghdadi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Abu_Mansur_al-Baghdadi) (d. 1037), the substitution theory was also applied to the death of [Ali ibn Abi Talib](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ali_ibn_Abi_Talib) by the semi-legendary 7th-century figure [Abdallah ibn Saba'](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Abdullah_ibn_Saba'); see [De Smet 2016](#CITEREFDe_Smet2016), pp. 98–99.

[^cite_note-562]: &#x20;On the writings attributed to al‐Mufaddal ibn Umar al‐Ju'fi, see [De Smet 2016](#CITEREFDe_Smet2016), p. 93. On the Brethren of Purity, see [Robinson 1991](#CITEREFRobinson1991), pp. 55–57, [Lawson 2009](#CITEREFLawson2009), pp. 129–133 and especially [De Smet 2016](#CITEREFDe_Smet2016), pp. 100–101. On the Isma'ili philosophers (who include [Abu Hatim al-Razi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Abu_Hatim_Ahmad_ibn_Hamdan_al-Razi), Abu Tammam, [Ja'far ibn Mansur al-Yaman](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ja'far_ibn_Mansur_al-Yaman), [Abu Yaqub al-Sijistani](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Abu_Yaqub_al-Sijistani) and [Ibrahim al-Hamidi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ibrahim_al-Hamidi)), see [Lawson 2009](#CITEREFLawson2009), pp. 123–129 and especially [De Smet 2016](#CITEREFDe_Smet2016), pp. 101–107. On al-Ghazali, see [Lawson 2009](#CITEREFLawson2009), pp. 117–118. This type of interpretation of Quran 4\:157–159 was specifically rejected by the Sunni Quran commentator al-[Baydawi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Qadi_Baydawi) (d. 1319); see [Lawson 2009](#CITEREFLawson2009), p. 155.
